# PERFECT-100 — honest audit and the roadmap to a genuinely finished SchoolOS

**Audit date:** 2026-09-12
**Scope:** the whole backend (`server/`, 5,593 lines of kernel + 29 plugin modules, 69 migrations) plus the frontend↔backend contract where it decides whether the backend's work is real to a user. 56 test files reviewed.
**Auditor:** 9 parallel deep-dive audits (security/tenancy, data & migrations, API contract, plugin architecture, RBAC, tests & gates, ops/observability/DR, product completeness) plus first-hand verification by the lead auditor.
**Baseline comparison:** `STILL NOT GOOD_1.md` (58/100 quality, 31/100 readiness) and `STILL NOT GOOD_2.md` (74/100 quality, 56/100 readiness, 2026-09-11). **Both are now stale** — several items they call missing have landed since (fee-plan doors, MFA doors, `force_change` enforcement, settings contract). This document judges the code as it is today, and says so where the older docs are wrong in either direction.

---

## 0. How to read this document

1. **§1** is the verdict: two honest grades, one weighted 100-point scorecard, and a plain answer to "is it 90% done?"
2. **§2** is what is genuinely good — do not rip this up. Most of the repair work below is *finishing*, not rewriting.
3. **§3** is the first structural defect (no organisation model) and **§4** the second (success-shaped silence). Together they generate roughly a third of the register, so fix them first.
4. **§5** is the findings register, in nine severity-ordered sections (5.1 security · 5.2 RBAC · 5.3 plugins · 5.4 data & migrations · 5.5 API contract · 5.6 product completeness · 5.7 tests & gates · 5.8 frontend coherence · 5.9 operations). Every row cites `path:line` and carries an ID you can quote back at me.
5. **§6** is the true "last 10%", itemised into 34 numbered items across five themes, each with an effort estimate.
6. **§7** is the execution plan — six phases with testable exit criteria, plus a ten-point definition of "finished".
7. **Appendix A** lists 23 facts I verified first-hand against the live code and database, so the spine of this document can be re-checked rather than trusted. **Appendix B** states the method, the limits, and exactly which four claims are labelled INFERENCE.

Honesty rules used throughout: a claim is only called *verified* if a line of code or a DB object was read. Inferences are labelled. Nothing here is taken from the project's own docs without re-checking the code — and where the code turned out to be *better* than the docs claim, the code is recorded as correct.

---

## 1. Verdict and scorecard

### 1.1 The two grades (same axes the project's own audits used, re-measured today)

| Grade | Baseline 1 | Baseline 2 (2026-09-11) | **Today** | What the number means |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A — Implementation quality** | 58 | 74 | **53 / 100** | How well the code that exists is built |
| **B — Enterprise production readiness** | 31 | 56 | **34 / 100** | Whether a paying school could run on it unattended |

**Read this before the numbers upset you: grade A going 74 → 55 is not a regression.** No one broke anything. The code today is *better* than it was at baseline 2 — several items those audits list as missing have since landed (§2, last paragraph). The number fell because this audit went deeper than the previous ones did. The earlier audits were architectural: they read source and reasoned about design. This one additionally read the **live database's actual ACL state** (`pg_roles`, `has_table_privilege`, `pg_default_acl`, `pg_policy`), and traced **complete exploit chains** rather than reviewing components. That found four things no source-only audit could see: the app's DB role owns every table *with `BYPASSRLS`*; the live grants contradict what two migrations claim to have revoked; a reset token is written in cleartext to a table six roles can read; and the event-lineage feed leaks the whole tenant's activity to students. So the honest reading is: **the earlier scores were measuring the design; this one measures the deployed reality.** Both numbers can be true at once, and the gap between them is itself a finding.

### 1.2 The weighted 100-point scorecard

A single number is useless unless the weights are stated and defensible. These weights reflect what actually determines whether an India-first school SIS survives a school term.

| # | Area | Weight | Score | Weighted | The one-line reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Security & multi-tenant isolation | 15 | **46** | 6.9 | Auth core is excellent; the privilege model underneath it is broken three ways, incl. a live owner-takeover path |
| 2 | Data model & integrity | 10 | **68** | 6.8 | Money and the ledger are right; no tenant-aligned FKs, 40 dangling references, no tamper-evident chain |
| 3 | API surface & contract quality | 8 | **54** | 4.3 | Locking and idempotency on money paths are right; there is no machine-readable contract at all |
| 4 | Plugin architecture & extensibility | 10 | **68** | 6.8 | The mechanics of plugin-ness are real and tested; the *contract* is unenforced documentation |
| 5 | RBAC, role taxonomy & least privilege | 12 | **52** | 6.2 | One real least-privilege engine exists and is unreachable across 173 of 214 doors; 9 of 29 plugins over-grant |
| 6 | Product completeness (can a school run a term) | 20 | **47** | 9.4 | Broad, real domain depth — but parents cannot actually be reached, teachers cannot mark a register, and five routes crash a real tenant |
| 7 | Frontend↔backend coherence & honesty | 10 | **42** | 4.2 | Client invents its own permission model, fabricates infra telemetry, and 44 k LOC are untested |
| 8 | Tests & engineering gates | 8 | **64** | 5.1 | Strong integration suite; a regression still merges, and the "green" numbers are stale |
| 9 | Operations, observability, DR | 7 | **44** | 3.1 | Cannot boot a fresh DB, no alerting, no scheduled backups, five workers never scheduled |
| | **Total** | **100** | | **52.9** | Weighted mean; rounds to **53** |

Areas 1–5 and 8–9 are scored by the dedicated audit lanes named in Appendix B. Areas **6 and 7** are my own assessment as lead auditor, grounded in the cited evidence of those lanes rather than a separate dedicated lane — I flag that so the provenance of every number is auditable.

### 1.3 The answer to "is it 90% complete?"

**No — and the reason matters more than the number.** By *surface area* you are closer to 90% than my score suggests: the domain breadth is genuinely impressive (29 plugins covering admissions, fees with refunds and reconciliation, exams with CBSE board policy, timetable, transport GPS, visitors, clinic, HR leave, PTM, safeguarding, DPDP/SAR, UDISE statutory export, Tally accounting and BI read models). Existing features are, on the whole, built to a much higher standard than "90% done" usually implies — money is integer paise throughout, webhooks verify HMACs over raw bytes and never settle money, and the audit ledger is hash-chained.

What you are at 90% is on **width**. What you are at ~53% is on **depth, trust and the last mile** — and the last mile is not more features. It is four specific things:

1. **Trust.** A school cannot run on a system where a parent can read every other child's attendance, where a `coordinator` can take over the owner's account, and where the audit ledger reports "gateway webhook verified" based on a regex match against a caller-supplied string. You can add every remaining feature and none of that changes.
2. **Reachability.** The features exist but the *people who should use them cannot*. Teachers are 403'd from the attendance register because no class-assignment model exists; the librarian account you asked for cannot be created because there is no `librarian` role and the plugin's own preset fails at both layers (§5.2 RBAC-3). This is the difference between "built" and "usable".
3. **Actual delivery.** Right now the platform's outbound communication — OTP, parent notices, absence alerts, dunning reminders — physically sends nothing by default (`logger` provider returns `{ok:true}`), and `/auth/otp/request` answers `{sent:true}` without a dispatch. A school's core promise to parents is a success-shaped no-op.
4. **Reproducibility.** The story of "it's almost done" is being told with numbers that no longer match the tree: the release certificate says 114/114 tests green against an actual 392, and the only full-run log on disk is red. You cannot finish the last 10% while the instrument panel is wrong.

**The genuine good news:** item 4 is cheap to fix, and items 1–3 are concentrated in a small number of root causes rather than scattered across 29 plugins. §3 and §4 name the two structural defects that generate most of the register; fix those two and roughly a third of the findings close together.

---

## 2. What is genuinely good (verified — protect this)

This is not a rescue job. The kernel is better than most funded products at this stage, and a rewrite would destroy real value. Every claim below is cited so it can be re-checked.

**Tenancy & data safety.** Transaction-scoped RLS via `SET LOCAL ROLE schoolos_app` plus `schoolos.tenant_id`/`schoolos.role` GUCs (`server/src/db.js:48-63`); 66 tables carry RLS policies, 53 of them `FORCE`d (verified live via `pg_class`). The app role holds no table-level DELETE in schema `app` (verified live via `role_table_grants`), so deletes are modelled deliberately. Student paternity/guardianship is structural (`family.child_link`) rather than name-matched where it was reviewed.

**Identity.** scrypt at N=2^17 with timing-equalized user-miss logins (so an unknown email costs the same as a known one), mandatory `jti` plus a server-side revocation denylist, `session_epoch` revoking sibling sessions on credential change, restricted `mustChange`/`mfaPending` sessions that can only reach the doors needed to finish the flow, CSRF double-submit for cookie mutations, and kernel-only OTP/MFA/reset vaults that the app role cannot read at all. Phone-OTP, Google SSO and multi-membership logins all 409 with a picker rather than silently taking `rows[0]`.

**Payments integrity.** Webhook HMACs are computed over raw wire bytes via an install-once content-type parser and compared with `timingSafeEqual`; replay is deduped on a unique insert so a replay is a 200 no-op; and webhooks **record facts only** — settlement requires an authenticated staff confirmation. This is the correct posture and it is rare.

**The audit ledger.** Append-only for the app role, written through a `SECURITY DEFINER` function that pins the tenant to the caller's GUC, hash-chained, with chain-preserving archival (copy-then-delete, never a bare DELETE) in the retention worker.

**The event spine.** A transactional outbox with at-least-once delivery, attempts counted before delivery, exponential backoff capped at 300s, poison rows parked with a reason, and `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` claiming so multiple relay replicas are safe. Per-tenant event budgets with a self-healing breaker are DB-backed, not in-process.

**Plugin mechanics.** A real drop-in loader mounts `src/plugins/<id>/` with zero kernel edits and fails the boot loudly rather than half-mounting. 169 of 171 plugin doors carry a per-request lifecycle gate, and gate presence is enforced by a CI scanner that fails on any unguarded or unjustified-public route. Enable/disable is owner/itadmin-gated and ledger-audited, and disable retains data by design.

**Engineering discipline.** 56 test files with per-file fresh databases; a permission-matrix CI gate; dependency-CVE and secret scanning wired to fail closed; a `check.js` that genuinely gates DB, migrations and RLS; digest-pinned non-root container; production config that refuses localhost DSNs, CORS `*`, short JWT secrets and non-strict seed guards; graceful shutdown with in-flight draining; defensive pool/statement/lock timeouts; and a backup script that verifies its own output with `pg_restore --list` plus a documented, dated restore drill.

**Honesty in the places that were audited before.** The fabricated AI telemetry row was removed (`app/src/components/Ai.tsx:56`), the MFA login challenge was fixed to a plain JSON contract (`server/src/app.js:207-215`), `force_change` is genuinely enforced (`:139-143, 217, 257`), MFA enrol/disable/verify doors exist (`:608, 625, 643`), fee-plan creation exists (`server/src/routes.js:511-558`), and the bootstrap grant matrix now revokes before granting `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` (`server/scripts/bootstrap.js:25-26, 32-50`). Several items the project's own older audits list as missing have since landed — which is why this document judges today's code, not theirs.

---

## 3. The one structural defect behind most of the others

**SchoolOS models a school as (a tenant, a pile of people, and free-text labels). It has no model of how a school is organised.** There is no class, no section, no subject, no teacher assignment, and therefore no notion of *who is responsible for what*. Every "role" in the system is global to the tenant; nothing is scoped to a thing.

This is not a code-quality problem. It is a missing domain model, and it is the reason the product feels 90% done: the parts that need *scope* cannot be finished on top of it.

### 3.1 The evidence

| Fact | Evidence |
|---|---|
| A student's class is two free-text columns, `grade` and `section`, with no FK and no owning entity. | psql: `information_schema.columns` → `app.students.grade text`, `app.students.section text` |
| There is no class, section, subject, or assignment table anywhere. | psql on `app`/`public` for `%assign%`, `%teacher%`, `%section%`, `%subject%` → **0 rows** (45 tables exist in `app`) |
| A timetable slot cannot reference a class or a real teacher: it stores `subject text` and `teacher_name text`, with FKs only to `period_id` and `tenant_id`. | `\d app.timetable_slots` |
| The role taxonomy is a global enum, duplicated in four places. | DB CHECK `users_role_check`; `server/src/auth.js:60`; `app/src/types/domain.ts:10`; `app/src/state/store.tsx:251-253` |

### 3.2 What that defect causes — every item traced to it

1. **Teachers cannot mark attendance at all.** The kernel's register door admits `owner, principal, coordinator, teacher`, then refuses everyone except owner/principal, with a comment admitting the reason: *"Class-assignment records don't exist yet, so the register can only be marked by the principal's office."* A real school cannot run this way for a single day. `server/src/routes.js:380-394`
2. **The same hole is still open in a plugin.** `POST /plugins/ops.timetable/attendance/autofill` admits `teacher, coordinator, principal` and writes attendance for *any* class and date with no assignment binding — so the plugin is simultaneously more permissive than the kernel and unauthorized in the same way the kernel just closed. `server/src/plugins/ops.timetable/index.js:107`
3. **The librarian problem.** Because there is no way to express "this person is responsible for the library", the library doors fall back to a hand-written staff list, `['owner','principal','coordinator','facilities']` — so a facilities staffer can issue and return books, and the principal is the de-facto librarian. `server/src/plugins/library/index.js:31`
4. **Plugin surfaces belong to nobody — surface authorization is a hand-maintained string list with exactly two tiers.** The client decides who sees what from a hardcoded 43-entry `staffOnly` array (`app/src/App.tsx:197`) and a single `light = ['parent','student'].includes(role)` test (`:191`). Every other role — owner, principal, coordinator, finance, admissions, transport, facilities, technician, teacher, itadmin — is lumped into one undifferentiated "staff" tier. `/library` sits in that list, so **the library screen is visible to every staff member of the tenant**, which is precisely the complaint "the library module is in the principal login". The code's own comment admits the list is maintained reactively, entry by entry, after each leak: *"/plugins, /settings, /library were missing and every parent/student route probe answered with the real screen"* and *"T509: /people-admin and /rails rendered for light roles until the API refused"*. The server-side mirror of this is that plugin nav is filtered by tenant *enablement* only, never by role (`app/src/plugins/useKernel.tsx:130-165`), and the plugin registry has no role field to filter on (`app/src/plugins/pluginRegistry.ts:55-84`).
5. **"Least privilege" is unimplementable in general.** The custom-role mask (`063_custom_roles.sql`) can only grant or deny whole URLs relative to one of the 12 global base roles. It cannot express "…for the classes they teach" or "…for the library they run". It is a patch over the missing model, and it is the right stepping stone, but it is not a substitute.
6. **Timetable, substitutions, exams and reports all key off strings.** Substitutions resolve by `teacher_name` (`routes.js:1186`), exam reports key off `grade`/`section` strings. A typo or a rename silently forks the data, and nothing prevents two teachers with the same name from colliding.
7. **Teacher-facing features die here too.** Homework, marks entry, and any "my classes" surface need the same missing entity; without it the teacher lane can only ever be a viewer of tenant-wide data.

### 3.3 The fix is small, and it unblocks the most

One migration plus one resolution layer is enough to change the shape of the whole product:

```
app.classes        (id, tenant_id, grade, section, name, class_teacher_id → app.users)
app.subjects       (id, tenant_id, name, code)
app.teaching_assignments (id, tenant_id, user_id → app.users, subject_id, class_id, is_primary)
```

Then:
- `students.class_id` becomes a real FK (keep `grade`/`section` as a denormal for compatibility and backfill from them).
- A **scope resolver** — `scopeFor(user)` returning the set of class ids a user owns/teaches (all for owner/principal/itadmin, none-as-global for teacher) — is added next to the existing `require()` gate. Doors then declare `requireRole(...)` **or** `requireScope('class', …)`, and the register door becomes: teacher may mark *their* classes, principal's office may mark any.
- The same resolver gives the librarian, nurse and warden cases a place to live: `scopeFor` gains `library`, `clinic`, `hostel` dimensions driven by a small `app.staff_scopes` table (`user_id, scope_type, scope_ref`), which is **the generalisation of `app.roles`** and what makes a 13th and 14th role unnecessary.
- Plugin visibility finally has something to gate on: the registry gains `requiresScope: 'library'`, and the nav filter (currently enablement-only) consults `scopeFor`.

Doing this converts four separate complaints (no librarian, teachers locked out of attendance, library in the principal's menu, staff-wide over-grants) into one coherent piece of work — and it is the only item in this document that pays for itself across every other area.

---

## 4. The second structural defect: success-shaped silence

The org-model gap (§3) is why whole *features* cannot be finished. This one is why the features that *do* exist cannot be trusted. The system reports success for work it did not do, in at least five independently verified places. Every one of them converts a small missing piece into a lie the customer acts on.

| # | What the product says | What actually happens | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| H1 | `POST /auth/otp/request` → `{ sent: true, expiresInSeconds, … }` | The response is built **before** the user is even looked up and **no rail is called anywhere** on this path. The OTP is written to the vault and logged as `OTP issued`; nothing is dispatched. On a real phone, the parent waits forever. | `server/src/app.js:297-310` |
| H2 | Approving a parent-notice action writes `status='delivered'`, `sent_at=now()`, `delivered_at=now()` | No rail call. The audit ledger and the `comms` event both assert "Parent notice SENT". | `server/src/routes.js:958-966` |
| H3 | The SMS rail returns `{ ok: true, provider: 'logger' }` | The default provider is `logger` — it writes a structured log line and returns success. The rail's own contract makes "logged" and "sent" indistinguishable, which is the mechanism that lets H1 and H2 be written honestly-looking. | `server/src/plugins/rails.sms/index.js:9-14, 81-90` |
| H4 | The IT-admin screen shows infrastructure telemetry: "Postgres primary · 14ms query p95 · Replication lag 0.2s · 3 read replicas", "Uptime · 30d 99.97%", "checked every 30s from the status worker" | There are no read replicas, `archive_mode` is off, no latency instrumentation exists in the request path, and no "status worker" exists in `server/scripts/`. | `app/src/screens/ItAdmin.tsx:47, 106, 108, 113` |
| H5 | The event queue's only observable metric reads "pending" | A failed (parked) row sets `processed_at = now()`, so **parking a poison message drives the queue-depth gauge down** — a total relay failure has the same metric signature as an idle healthy system. | `server/scripts/outbox-worker.js:74`; gauge at `server/src/app.js:957-962` |

Why this class matters more than its individual severity: the customer's own words are "it's 90% done but not perfect". In a product that lies about delivery, a school cannot tell the difference between "the circular went out" and "the circular silently didn't", so **every** feature inherits the uncertainty. It is also the cheapest class in this document to fix — each item is hours, not weeks — and the highest-leverage for perceived quality.

The fix pattern is one decision applied five times: **make the default honest, and let configuration add capability.**
1. A `communications` row starts at `status='queued'`; only a provider receipt moves it to `sent`, and only a provider callback moves it to `delivered`. If no rail is configured, the action **fails loudly** ("no SMS provider is configured — connect one in Settings") instead of succeeding quietly.
2. `/auth/otp/request` enqueues a real comms row and reports `sent: false, reason: 'no provider configured'` when it cannot dispatch; it must never claim `sent: true` before it has a provider receipt. Until then the dev echo remains the only honest path, and the UI must say so.
3. The `logger` provider reports `{ ok: true, provider: 'logger', delivered: false }` — or is renamed `log-only` — so no caller can mistake it for delivery.
4. Infrastructure telemetry on any screen reads from `/metrics` or is deleted. A number with no source is a defect, not a placeholder.
5. Parked rows get their own counter and column (`parked_at`), and the pending gauge excludes them, so failure and idleness look different.

---

## 5. Findings register

Severity: **P0** = exploitably wrong or breaks trust in money/child data · **P1** = privilege escalation, a broken promise to the user, or blocks production · **P2** = hardening/quality, will hurt at scale · **P3** = polish. Every row was read in source; the four audits behind this register ran read-only.

### 5.1 Security & multi-tenant isolation — **46/100**

Three lanes audited this area. Two things they proved by *doing*, not reading: **there is no SQL injection** (all 578 template-literal query arguments were parsed — 550 carry `$N` placeholders, 25 are fully static, and the only 3 interpolations are module constants or server-derived schema probes; no route accepts a `sort`/`order`/`column` parameter) and **there is no path traversal** (uploads never use the client filename on disk; downloads reject `..` and absolute paths before `join`). The RLS layer itself is also real and empirically enforced: as `schoolos_app` with a foreign tenant GUC the row count is 0, and with the GUC unset it is 0 (fail-closed, not fail-open), across 99 of 100 tables with owner-scoping correctly written `AS RESTRICTIVE`. The score is dragged down by the privilege model *underneath* that layer — three independent defects, each sufficient to fail an enterprise review.

| ID | Sev | Finding | Evidence | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SEC-0 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8); was the most severe finding in this review** | **A live password-reset token is persisted in cleartext in `app.communications.body` and served to six tenant roles, giving a complete escalation to tenant superuser.** The chain, verified end-to-end in source by me: (1) the reset mail body embeds the raw token — `Your one-time reset code is: ${raw}` (`app.js:547`); (2) the token is inserted into `auth.reset_tokens` **hashed**, so the DB holds no copy — but the *message body* is persisted to the comms ledger, and redaction is conditional on the delivery mode: `const ledgerBody = r.sandbox ? body : body.replace(raw, …)` (`app.js:557`); (3) `sandbox` is the **default and permanent** state for any tenant without SMTP credentials, because the provider defaults to `logger` (`rails.email/index.js:145`) which returns `{ ok: true, sandbox: true }` (`:152`) — and `sandbox:true` is returned by *every* failure path too (`:154`), so this is not a developer-machine edge case; (4) `GET /communications` runs `SELECT *` on that table and is open to `owner, principal, coordinator, finance, itadmin, transport` (`routes.js:1356-1360`); (5) the *request* door is unauthenticated (`app.js:515`) and redemption needs only the token, setting the password and bumping `session_epoch` (`app.js:572-592`). **Net: a `coordinator`, `transport`, `finance`, `principal` or `itadmin` — none of them owner-equivalent — triggers a reset for the owner's address, reads the token out of the tenant-visible ledger, and sets the owner's password.** No cryptography is broken and nothing is logged as anomalous. The code comment concedes the design ("SANDBOX sends keep the full body … acceptable on developer machines only") but nothing enforces that qualifier. | `server/src/app.js:515, 547, 552-563, 572-592`; `server/src/plugins/rails.email/index.js:139-154`; `server/src/routes.js:1356-1360` | (1) **Always** redact — the ledger is a delivery *proof*, not a delivery *channel*; redact on the token's provenance, never on a delivery-mode flag. (2) Give `/communications` an explicit column list omitting `body`; move body reads to a separate owner/itadmin door. (3) Regression assertion: no `PASSWORD_RESET` row's body may match `/^[0-9a-f]{64}$/`. (4) Alert on any reset requested for an `owner` account. |
| SEC-1 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8): `q()` now runs as `schoolos_app` (no BYPASSRLS); 103 privileged sites made explicit; boundary test + regression probe** | **RLS is not a boundary.** The API pool connects as the `schoolos` role, which owns all ~100 tables **and has `BYPASSRLS`** (verified live in `pg_roles`). RLS therefore applies only inside `withUser()` (which drops to the non-bypass `schoolos_app`); every bare `q()` call is unconstrained — roughly 40 request-path queries plus 37 plugin sites. Isolation rests entirely on developers remembering the wrapper, and `/metrics` already reads `app.outbox`/`app.communications`/`app.events` with no tenant predicate. (The RLS layer itself is genuinely well built — 99/100 tables, `AS RESTRICTIVE` owner-scoping, fail-closed with the GUC unset — which is what makes the bypass so consequential.) | `server/src/db.js:6,22`; live `pg_roles` (`schoolos rolbypassrls=t`, `schoolos_app rolbypassrls=f`); `server/src/app.js:957` | Give the pool a dedicated non-owner login with `NOBYPASSRLS`; `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` on the 40 tables that lack it; scope `/metrics` by tenant or move it to an operator guard; CI lint failing any tenant-table query issued outside `withUser` |
| SEC-2 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8)** | **A custom role can grant every door in the system, including owner-only doors.** `MASK_RE` accepts `* /*`; `validateMask` only requires that each pattern match *some* registered route; `ROLES_ADMIN = ['owner','itadmin']`; and `require()`'s else-branch treats an `allow` match as sufficient to lift a role refusal. So an **itadmin** — not owner-equivalent — can mint a grant-all role and assign it to a staff account, after which every door passes while audit rows still show the base role. Mask *grants* write no audit row at all. | `server/src/routes.js:84, 101-124, 163, 24` | Cap `allow` masks to a subset of the *authoring caller's own* permitted doors; forbid `base_role:'owner'` for itadmin-authored roles; add a mask-proof floor for roles/plugins/api-keys/audit/metrics; audit every mask that grants against a base-role refusal |
| SEC-17 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8, migration 074)** | **A policy could RAISE instead of DENY.** 94 predicates inlined `current_setting(...)`: 60 could raise **22P02** on an empty-string GUC and 34 could raise **42704** when the GUC was never set. Same "no tenant context" state denied on most tables and hard-errored on others — an outage reachable from a connection-state quirk. Now normalised through `app.current_tenant()`, with a catalogue-wide test that fails if any policy inlines it again. |
| SEC-20 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (migration 075 + tests/audit-integrity.test.js)** | **`app.audit_append` accepts `p_hash` and stores it VERBATIM while validating only `p_prev_hash`** (`054:114-120`), and `schoolos_app` holds EXECUTE on it — so any app-role caller can write a ledger row whose hash it chose. The chain still LINKS, so ordinary link-checking sees nothing. **Proven live, not theoretical:** recomputing every row from its own columns reproduces **27 of 53**; for row `migration 013 (plugin factory)` the stored hash matches the MIGRATION FILE's `outcome` literal exactly, not the `outcome` the row now contains — i.e. that row was altered after insertion, detectable only by recomputation. Because the 403 at `:181` is payload-dependent, an UNSIGNED request naming an order id that resolves returns **401** while a non-existent one returns **403** — an unauthenticated tenant/payment-existence and rail-configuration oracle, plus a timing oracle. | SEC-2b | **P0/P1 — DELETE half CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8); UPDATE/SELECT half open** | **The project's own D-35 grant law is violated in the live database, and a landmine re-creates the violation.** Verified first-hand: `schoolos_app` holds `DELETE` on **12 tables** including `plugin_razorpay.order_log` and `plugin_razorpay.webhook_log` (migration 054's checksum matches disk, so its `REVOKE` provably ran — the control state is proven, the mechanism is not); it holds `UPDATE` on **`app.users`** (which carries `password_hash` and `role`) and on `app.enrollment_history` ("written once, never edited") and `app.files`; and it can `SELECT app.webhook_subscriptions` (whose `sign_secret` signs every outbound webhook for a tenant). Root cause: a **stray `postgres`-owned default ACL** `{schoolos_app=arwd/postgres}` in schema `app` grants full DML **including DELETE** to the app role on every future table — it appears in no repo file and sits outside the migration checksum ledger, so it silently defeats migration 054's "future tables are born with no app-role grants". | SEC-4 | **P1 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (tests/cases-privacy.test.js 11/11, mutation-probed; the code fix predated this closure and the GATE WAS THE MISSING PIECE)** | `POST /cases` admits **every** authenticated role, and lets the caller choose `priority`, `owner` and `slaHours` — so a parent or student can inject fabricated critical cases into the staff board and event spine. The read and status doors both enforce `STAFF_CASE_ROLES`. | `server/src/routes.js:811` vs `:789-791, 826-828` | Apply `STAFF_CASE_ROLES`; force `origin:'staff'`  CLOSED: the four staff decisions surrendered to the request body are now DERIVED from the caller's role — `origin` (a family report can no longer label itself staff/system and misattribute itself in the audit spine and on the staff board), `owner`, `priority` ('critical' is refused to non-staff outright, because every parent marking their own case critical makes severity meaningless and floods the board) and `slaHours` (clamped to a week for a family report, so a parent cannot set an SLA that never comes due). **The register's recommendation was deliberately NOT taken:** gating the door on STAFF_CASE_ROLES would have broken a tested product flow — a parent must be able to report a bullying incident — so the door stayed open to families and the FIELDS were closed instead. The real gap was that the pre-existing test SENT `origin:'parent'` and asserted 'parent' back, which passes whether the door derives the field or trusts it: **a test that supplies its own expected value cannot detect the door ceasing to derive it.** Four attack-shaped tests now send what a hostile caller would send, and removing the derivation fails 4 of 11. |

| SEC-6 | **P1 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (validation) · residual SPLIT OUT as SEC-22** | `/plugins/:id/settings` persisted **arbitrary client JSON** as a plugin's settings. The manifest validator existed but was wired only into `/plugins/:id/settings-form/validate` — the door that deliberately persists **nothing** — so the check ran on the path that writes nothing and was skipped on the path that writes everything. Those settings drive gateway keys, webhook HMAC secrets, replay windows and OTP lifetimes, so the product's most security-sensitive configuration accepted any shape of anything. **Two further defects were found while fixing it and are recorded separately:** the schema being validated against was itself stale (SEC-22), and secrets remain plaintext + verbatim-returned (SEC-23). | `server/src/routes.js` (settings door) · `src/settings-contract.js:31,97` · `src/plugin-catalog.js` · `src/plugins/admissions.funnel/manifest.js` · `migrations/084_settings_pattern_escape.sql` | **CLOSED 2026-09-14.** The door now calls `validateSettingsAgainstSchema(canonicalSchema(prevRow.manifest), body)` **before** the UPDATE and persists only `check.normalized`, so defaults are materialised and unvalidated values never reach the column. An **undeclared key is a 400, not a silent drop** — discarding part of a credential payload is the worse failure, because a school that typos a key name would see success and keep using the old secret. A **refused save writes nothing at all**, including its valid keys (asserted by snapshotting stored settings across the refusal); a door that validated per-key while writing anyway would silently change live configuration while reporting failure. **Pinned by** `server/tests/sec6-settings-contract.test.js` (6/6): undeclared key rejected + nothing written, wrong type rejected, out-of-range (`webhook_freshness_mins 0` — which would mean "accept events of any age") rejected, normalized storage, the admissions-window regression guard, and the no-schema pass-through asserted **at the validator** because all 30 shipped modules declare a schema and the branch is unreachable through the door (documented, not faked). **Mutation-probed both ways:** enforcement disabled → 3 of 6 fail; manifest reconcile disabled → the regression guard fails. |
| SEC-7 | **P1 — CLOSED (verified 2026-09-14)** | Webhook replay windows were **optional**: both rails wrapped the freshness check in `if (ts != null)`, so a signed payload that omitted the timestamp was accepted with no window, and dedupe rested on event id alone. | `payments.upi/index.js:114`; `payments.razorpay/index.js:273` | **CLOSED.** Both rails now reject a missing timestamp outright (`if (ts == null) return 400 … "freshness cannot be established"`), so replay defence is no longer opt-in **by the sender**. Already **pinned before this session** by `server/tests/webhook-preauth.test.js:179-190` ("SEC-21: freshness cannot be skipped by omitting the timestamp") — a signed event with no timestamp is refused, *and* a companion test proves the check is not simply always-fail by exercising the `body.timestamp` fallback. Verified green: 8/8. The register had this row open against code that was already correct; the pin, not the code, was the missing piece. |
| SEC-8 | **P1 — CLOSED 2026-09-14** | `/fees/payments/:id/confirm` marked a payment *"webhook-verified"* by **regex-matching a caller-supplied** gateway reference (`/^[A-Z0-9-]{10,}$/`), and the refund path synthesised references of the same shape — so the product manufactured strings that satisfied its own evidence check. The ledger's provenance claim was a property of a string's **shape**, not of any gateway record. | `server/src/routes.js` (`/fees/payments/:id/confirm`) | **CLOSED.** `webhookVerified` is now `verifiedBy !== null`, where `verifiedBy` names the rail whose **own record** contains the reference: a `SELECT 1 FROM plugin_razorpay.order_log WHERE … AND payment_id = $1 AND (razorpay_order_id = $2 OR razorpay_payment_id = $2) LIMIT 1` inside `withUser` — RLS-scoped to the tenant **and** joined to this payment, so neither the tenant nor the payment can be substituted. `order_log` is written with the gateway's `razorpay_order_id` at order creation and updated with the gateway-issued `razorpay_payment_id` by the **signature-verified** webhook, so a caller-invented reference matches nothing. The audit action/reason now name the rail that vouched. Settling a payment with no gateway evidence is **still permitted** (a desk taking cash must be able to) but is labelled *"manual reconciliation — no gateway evidence"*. **Documented limit, not a claim:** `app.payments` carries `CHECK (status <> 'confirmed' OR gateway_ref IS NOT NULL)`, so a confirmed row **must** have a reference and the column cannot record its absence — the reference is stored, and the **verification method** is what distinguishes it. Nulling it was tried first and failed with a raw `23514` on the money path; the honest reading is that the column records *what the desk typed* while `verifiedBy` records *whether a gateway vouched*. **Pinned by** `server/tests/sec8-payment-provenance.test.js` (4/4): a fabricated `MANUAL-1234567` (shaped exactly as the old regex accepted) is **not** recorded as verified; a genuine provider reference **is** (so the fix is not a blanket downgrade); a real reference belonging to **another payment** cannot launder this one; and **another tenant's** coherent gateway record cannot verify this tenant's payment. **Mutation-probed:** restoring the regex fails the attack test. No regressions: 40/40 across `fee-plans`, `payments-refund`, `tally`, `api`, `security`. |

| SEC-18 | **P2 — CLOSED 2026-09-16 (parser mounted once at the kernel; name-match download leg removed)** | **A declared upload cap is unenforceable by construction.** Both `files.store` and `people.admin` guard their multipart mount with the *same* `Symbol.for('schoolos.multipart.mounted')`, so the first registrant wins and the second's limits never apply. The loader sorts alphabetically, so `files.store`'s 5 MiB parser limit lands and `people.admin`'s documented 2 MiB CSV cap is dead code — the effective limit is an emergent property of plugin load order (the file's own comments concede it). Additionally, the file-download authorization has a **name-match** clause: alongside the structural `family.child_link` leg there is a `UNION ALL` leg granting access when the caller's *account name* equals a guardian row's name, so a tenant user whose account is named after a real guardian can download that child's files. Tenancy is not breached (RLS holds), but this is the exact name-equality anti-pattern the project hardened against elsewhere. | `files.store/index.js:43, 87-90, 219-233`; `people.admin/index.js:37, 47-53, 515-525`; `plugins/loader.js:51` | Register `@fastify/multipart` **once** from `app.js` with explicit limits and pass per-route limits via `req.parts({limits})`, plus a code-enforced cap; make the parent access leg structural only (`family.child_link` or a verified guardian-phone match) — delete the name-match leg |

| SEC-22 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (found while fixing SEC-6)** | **The schema every plugin validator validates against was STALE, and the module's real declaration sat unread on disk.** `app.plugins.manifest` is written by the DDL when a migration creates the row (`018_admissions_funnel.sql:47` inlines a manifest), while `ensurePluginCatalog`'s INSERT is guarded by `WHERE NOT EXISTS` — it **only ever writes a manifest when it CREATES the row**. In the seeded world the row always exists first (`scripts/seed.js` replays the migration SQL and only *then* calls the catalog), so the DDL's manifest survives permanently and the module's own `manifest.js` is never applied. Measured on a freshly seeded database: `admissions.funnel`'s stored `cycle_start` pattern was **over-escaped** — migration 022 writes `^\\d{2}-\\d{2}$` through two escaping layers, producing a 15-character value that matches a **literal backslash followed by `d`** and therefore never matches a real `MM-DD` (the correct form is 13 chars). The three affected keys are `admission_year`, `cycle_start`, `cycle_end` — the session year stamped on every lead and the window deciding whether admissions are open. Invisible while nothing validated the write path; the moment SEC-6 enforced validation, the plugin **rejected its own valid settings** and its session window became unconfigurable — a worse failure than the one being fixed. | `server/src/plugin-catalog.js:76-90`; `migrations/022_admissions_india_semantics.sql:39-41`; `migrations/084_settings_pattern_escape.sql`; `server/src/plugins/admissions.funnel/manifest.js` | **CLOSED — and it needed BOTH halves; I deleted this fix once believing the manifest edit alone sufficed, and fresh databases kept failing.** (1) `ensurePluginCatalog` now reconciles the stored `settingsSchema` against the module's file manifest, scope deliberately narrow — **only the schema subtree**, because six plugins (`payments.razorpay`, `payments.upi`, `comms.whatsapp`, `accounting.tally`, `transport.gps`, `ops.timetable`) have a stored schema their file manifest does **not** declare, so a whole-manifest overwrite would have silently deleted six working schemas. No write when the two already agree. (2) `migrations/084` repairs the over-escaped patterns **already in the database** — necessary because the catalog reconcile does not run at boot, so existing tenants (`meridian` in every test database) keep the DDL row. 084 is written with `chr(92)` throughout rather than backslash literals, because reasoning about which of SQL's/JSON's/psql's escaping layers consumes `'\\'` is exactly how the original defect was introduced and I got it wrong twice; it is idempotent, and its verification is conditional on rows existing because `app.plugins` is **empty** during migrations (seed runs after migrate) — an earlier version asserted a row must be present, aborted on every fresh database, and took the whole admissions suite down through its `before` hook. **Pinned by** `tests/sec6-settings-contract.test.js` (admissions-window regression guard) and the manifest comments explaining the escaping so the obvious next "fix" cannot reintroduce it. **Mutation-probed:** reconcile disabled → the guard fails. |
| SEC-23 | **P1 — CLOSED 2026-09-16** | Secret-class plugin settings are encrypted with a tenant-bound AES-GCM envelope before persistence, decrypted only inside provider/kernel paths, and masked from privileged and non-privileged registry responses. Legacy plaintext values converge on the next catalog/settings write; masked placeholders cannot overwrite untouched credentials. | `server/src/settings-crypto.js`; `server/src/routes.js`; `server/tests/settings-crypto.test.js` | Add key-version rotation when an external KMS is introduced |

### 5.2 RBAC, role taxonomy & least privilege — **52/100**

| ID | Sev | Finding | Evidence | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RBAC-1 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8): enforcement is shared by both gates, rides `authenticate`, and `canReach` lifts in-handler role checks** | **Custom-role masks are silently ignored on every plugin door.** Enforcement lives only in `applyCustomMask`, called from the kernel `require` and `me`. The plugin gate (`app.js:783`) never calls it — while the mask *validator* matches against plugin URLs, so `POST /plugins/library/loans` validates and is then never consulted. The one least-privilege mechanism is blind to 173 of 214 doors. | `server/src/routes.js:20,24,35`; `server/src/app.js:783-790` | One shared authorizer used by both gates; regression test asserting a deny mask on a plugin door 403s |
| RBAC-1c | **P2 — OPEN (the mask ceiling is blind to `me`-gated doors, and the converted checks are asymmetric)** | The mask authoring ceiling (`MASK_AMP`) validates a proposed allow-list against the AUTHOR's own reach, but it can only inspect doors whose gate stamped the door's role set: the two `me`-gated doors converted by RBAC-1 (`GET /fees/obligations`, `GET /cases`) record `null`, so the ceiling never fires for them. An owner/IT-admin authored mask naming `GET /cases` therefore lets any account holding that role — including one based on `parent` — read every complaint in the school, where before RBAC-1 the built-in-role check refused regardless of the mask. Both present authors are members of every affected door set, so this is a widening behind a blind guardrail rather than a low-privilege escalation (independent review could not construct a low-privilege case). The same review found the mirror-image asymmetry: `teacher.support`'s close door and `files.store`'s list door still use raw role checks, so a mask-granted role can now read the whole safety box but not close a report, and download any file but not list others'. | `server/src/custom-roles.js` (ceiling), `server/src/routes.js` (fee ledger, both case-log refusals), `teacher.support` close, `files.store` list | Stamp the door's role set on every sensitive `me`-gated door, or move the ceiling to a door registry; then finish the conversion so the read and write halves of one surface agree. |
| OPS-5b | **P2 — OPEN (residual registration oracle on UNSCOPED OTP requests)** | The scoped path now answers a channel-less school identically for every number and the response keeps a constant key set, but an UNSCOPED request (`POST /auth/otp/request` with no `tenantId`) cannot ask the channel question before the lookup, so a known number at a channel-less school answers 503 while a stranger answers the uniform 200. Independent review also PROVED the narrower surviving form: with the rail ENABLED, a stranger answers `channel:null` while a registered number answers `channel:'sms'`, because delivery evidence is inherently existence-revealing (that is the whole point of OPS-5) — closing it would mean the door no longer tells a real user whether a code is coming — the same tradeoff the reset door documents, and the login door already answers 409 `multiTenant` for known identities. Refusals are now logged with the tenant, so an operator can see them. | `server/src/app.js` (`/auth/otp/request`) | Decide the channel at platform level when no tenant is named (so the refusal is uniform there too), or make the sign-in UI always send `tenantId`. |
| RBAC-2b | **P2 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8 round 21): the safety box could be ASSIGNED to a role its own doors refuse** | Narrowing the box readers to the safeguarding tier left the assignment filter at "any active staff role except teacher/parent/student", so a report could be parked on the finance office and marked `assigned` with an audit row naming them — while both the read queue and the close door refused that person at the gate. An assignee who can never act, on a child's protection report. Found by independent review; fixed by making the assignable set the SAME list as the reader set (`BOX_READERS`), so the two cannot drift apart again, and by making the close door's authority check mask-aware (`canReach`) like the other converted sites. Verified by `tests/rbac-counselor.test.js` (7/7), which now files a report as a student, refuses finance as an assignee, assigns a counsellor and proves that counsellor can read what was assigned. | `server/src/plugins/teacher.support/index.js` (assign + close) | Closed. |
| PLG-15 | **P2 — CLOSED 2026-09-16** | The demo seed now applies the same `DEFAULT_ON` catalog state as signup after catalog reconciliation, without changing the non-destructive behavior for existing tenants. | `server/scripts/seed.js`; `server/src/plugin-catalog.js` | Keep seeded-vs-signup catalog parity coverage |
| DAT-15 | **P2 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8 round 21): a phone number stored as typed is a phone number that can never sign in** | The people console stored `b.phone.trim()` verbatim while both OTP doors and the vault now key on the normalised E.164 form, so a number saved as `+91 98450 00101` — the format the seed itself uses — would have been answered with the uniform "sent" and delivered nothing, forever, with no error anywhere. The console now normalises on write through the project's single normaliser and refuses what cannot be a phone (at the desk, with the operator present). The same review pass found `toE164` rejecting the 11-digit Indian trunk form `09845000108`, which is how the number is written on a form; it is now accepted, alongside the 13-digit `091…` form. | `server/src/plugins/people.admin/index.js`, `server/src/plugins/_shared/phone.js` | Closed; the normaliser's table is in its header comment. |
| PLG-14 | **P1 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (§8): catalog-seeded rows carry each plugin's REAL manifest** | `ensurePluginCatalog` wrote `{id, version}` as the manifest for every row, and the plugin manager reads its settings form out of that manifest — so every tenant provisioned by signup or by the `GET /plugins` self-heal, i.e. every tenant created since the catalog landed, showed "Nothing to configure" for all 29 plugins and could never point a rail at a provider. The catalog now resolves `plugins/<id>/manifest.js` (22 of 29 by module, the rest through the same loader), and a test asserts every `CATALOG` entry resolves a manifest with an id plus — for a catalog-seeded tenant — a real `settingsSchema` in `app.plugins`. | `server/src/plugin-catalog.js`, `server/tests/rails-catalog-otp.test.js` | Closed. A future plugin shipped without a `manifest.js` now fails the suite instead of seeding a dead form. |
| RBAC-1b | **P2 — OPEN (scope/SoD checks are deliberately NOT mask-liftable)** | After RBAC-1, a custom-role ALLOW lifts a role refusal on plugin doors, on doors gated by `authenticate`, and on the **in-handler permission checks** that `canReach` was applied to (fees ledger, staff case log + status change, file download admin, safeguarding authority). The remaining role comparisons in handlers are of two kinds and were left alone on purpose: **scope/ownership** (`con.filed_by === req.user.id || …`, `box.assigned_to === …`, `p.owner_id === …`, the parent→child branch of `/fees/obligations`, `files.store`'s `scoped` download set) and **segregation of duties** (`sodFallback`, the action `ownerOk` check, session-year change, the visitor override). A mask grant answers "may you reach this door", never "is this row yours" or "may you also be the checker", so lifting them would be an authorization bug, not a fix. 36 role comparisons remain in handlers across 6 files (`routes.js` 15, `teacher.support` 11, `files.store` 3, `app.js` 2, `admissions.funnel` 2, `ops.visitors` 1, `custom-roles.js`/`app.js` gate internals excluded); each needs the permission-vs-scope judgement this row records, not a mechanical replacement. | `server/src/routes.js` (15 sites), `server/src/plugins/teacher.support/index.js` (11), `server/src/plugins/files.store/index.js:270`, `server/src/plugins/admissions.funnel/index.js:80,137`, `server/src/plugins/ops.visitors/index.js:300`, `server/src/app.js:696` | Classify each site as permission (use `canReach`) or scope/SoD (leave), with a test per site that a mask grant DOES and DOES NOT change. |

| PLG-1 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (§8 round 23–24)** | `permissions[]` is enforced. (1) All **29** manifests declare their needs — the seven that declared nothing (`library`, `ops.visitors`, `exams.assessment`, `files.store`, `pack.cbse`, `pack.dpdp`, `people.admin`) declare the facilities their own code calls, derived from a static scan of that code, and the catalogue's copy-pasted `memory.write` for the two packs is deleted rather than enshrined because no path touches `app.memory_entries`; (2) `server/src/plugin-permissions.js` holds ONE frozen vocabulary, the seam map that records in code which permissions have no enforcement seam yet (`unenforced:*` is debt, not a claim), the manifest resolver and a single refusal shape; (3) **the enable door stores the manifest's declaration and no longer reads `req.body.permissions` at all** — the cheat this project demonstrated on itself (`enable library with permissions:['students.read']` → 200) is closed in BOTH directions, and the test proves a caller cannot widen the grant either; (4) **every door refuses** while the stored grant does not cover the declaration, in the shared gate 27 of 29 plugins already use, naming the missing permission and returning `PERMISSION_DENIED`; (5) the catalogue reconciles stored rows to their manifest, so enforcement answers to the grant rather than to a stale cache. Evidence: `tests/plg-permissions.test.js` **5/5** (including a sweep over all 29 manifests) and `rails-catalog-otp.test.js` **9/9 in both postures**. The B2 regression that blocked closure was not caused by the enforcement: the test deleted only `rails.sms` while asserting "a school with **no** channel", so it had been passing on test-order accident, and its `sent:false` claim was asserted in the dev-echo branch where the uniform "sent" body is the anti-oracle design. Fixture and assertion placement corrected — both strengthened, nothing weakened. Residual (registered as PLG-1b): `scopedKernel` — making an undeclared `emitEvent`/`requestAudit` a hard error — is designed and not yet wired. | `server/src/plugin-permissions.js` (new), `src/routes.js`, `src/plugins/_shared/lifecycle-gate.js`, `src/plugin-catalog.js`, 7 manifests, `tests/plg-permissions.test.js` (new) | Closed. |
| PLG-1b | **P2 — CLOSED 2026-09-16 (wired at the loader; a call-site test proves refusal + audit)** | Three permissions have a real seam (`audit.request` → `auditLog`, `events.emit` → `emitEvent`, `comms.send` → the rails) and `PERMISSION_SEAM` says so in code. Wiring `scopedKernel(kernel, pluginId, declared)` into the loader would make an undeclared facility call a hard error plus an audit row, converting those three from "declared" into "enforced at the call site" rather than only at the door. The other thirteen permissions are honestly marked `unenforced:raw-sql` — a plugin reads tables directly, and closing that needs per-door annotations (168 doors) that were rejected in design as unreviewable. | `server/src/plugins/loader.js`, `src/plugin-permissions.js` | **CLOSED:** the loader now hands every plugin `scopedKernel(kernel, id, manifest.permissions)`, and `tests/next10-followups.test.js` drives the facade directly: an undeclared `auditLog`/`requestAudit` call rejects with `PERMISSION_DENIED` **and writes the denial to the ledger**, a declared `emitEvent` is stamped `emittedBy:'plugin:<id>'` even when the caller forges `{emittedBy:'kernel'}`, and a declared `requestAudit` stamps `actorRole:'plugin:<id>'`. The 13 `unenforced:*` permissions remain recorded debt by design, not an implied close. |


| PLG-2 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (§8 round 24)** | `entitlement` is checked. Design decision recorded, because it is a product decision and not a technical finding: the plan is a fact about the TENANT (`app.tenants.plan`, **migration 080**, `NOT NULL DEFAULT 'trial'` + a CHECK over the six plan keys), and the plan→plugin mapping is ONE authored matrix in `server/src/entitlements.js`. The stored `app.plugins.entitlement` values could not be the gate: they hold six mutually inconsistent vocabularies. `DEFAULT 'trial'` includes everything, which is why this breaks nothing (the 36 files that call an enable door, the demo tenant and every signup tenant keep working) while the column, the constraint and both refusal paths become real — defaulting to a restricted plan would have turned a P0 fix into an outage. Enforcement: (1) the **enable door returns 402 before any write**, so an unentitled enable leaves nothing half-created, and the test asserts the row is byte-identical afterwards; (2) the **shared request gate checks the plan per request**, so a plan DOWNGRADE revokes access even while the row still says `enabled` — and the row is deliberately not rewritten, because access follows the plan and the ledger keeps its history. Evidence: `tests/plg-entitlement.test.js` **5/5**, including a completeness sweep asserting every catalogue plugin appears in a plan (a new plugin with no plan is a plugin no school could ever enable) and that the CHECK constraint really refuses an invented plan name. Honest residuals, registered rather than implied: `trial` has no expiry (this means "unassigned = everything", not a timed trial); the UI availability surface (`GET /plugins` marking what the plan includes) is not done; and of the four documented enable gates, `dependsOn` and kernel-compat remain unimplemented. | `server/migrations/080_tenant_plan.sql`, `src/entitlements.js` (new), `src/routes.js`, `src/plugins/_shared/lifecycle-gate.js`, `tests/plg-entitlement.test.js` (new) | Closed. |

The declaration was corrected to `@1.0.1` and a new test (`tests/next10-followups.test.js`) asserts every machine-readable dependency names the version its target actually ships, so the next drift fails CI instead of silently disabling a plugin. |

### 5.4 Data model, integrity & migrations — **68/100**

The bones are good: money is **integer paise end-to-end with no float anywhere** (live `information_schema` check found zero `numeric`/`float` money columns), one-open-payment-per-obligation is a partial unique index rather than a code check, the ledger is INSERT-denied to the app role and written through a tenant-pinned `SECURITY DEFINER` appender, the chain's LINKS are intact live (53 rows, 0 broken links) — but that measured links, not hashes: recomputation shows **26 of those 53 rows do not reproduce**, one of them provably altered after insertion (SEC-20), every queue uses `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` with counted attempts, backoff and in-transaction parking, and two concurrent migration runners cannot double-apply thanks to a session advisory lock plus per-file transactions and sha256 drift detection. It is dragged down by four structural defects.

| ID | Sev | Finding | Evidence | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| DAT-1 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-14 (migration 081 + tests/dat1-tenant-alignment.test.js, 6/6)** | **Tenant alignment is not enforced by the schema.** Every child FK referenced the parent's PK only, never `(tenant_id, id)`. RLS prevents *reading* a mismatch, so a cross-tenant child row was invisible rather than impossible — it could sit with `tenant_id=A` and `student_id∈B`, absent from both tenants' ledgers. **The register's example list is illustrative, not the work set: MEASURED, 57 conversions across 44 child tables and 26 parents.** `app.tenants` is out of scope by construction (it has no `tenant_id` column, so a tenant cannot be cross-tenant). THE MECHANISM CANNOT FIRE.** `ON DELETE CASCADE` needs somebody to delete the parent, and **nothing in this codebase ever deletes a student or a user** — no `DELETE FROM app.students` anywhere in src/, scripts/ or migrations/, and `app.students.status` is CHECK-constrained to `active\|alumni\|transferred` (all 375 live students are `active`). Erasure here is redaction by design, so the proposed cascade is a fix-shaped object. **3. THE DIAGNOSIS.** The columns that survive an erasure are ON the parent (`app.students.name` / `guardian_name` / `guardian_phone`), which no child-side `ON DELETE` can reach — and the existing suite concedes it at `tests/l2-dpdp-erasure.test.js:119` ("office-lane surfaces untouched … documented gap"). Feasibility MEASURED, not assumed: every reference trialled inside a SAVEPOINT against a migrated+seeded database with a negative control, then applied for real with zero violations. | DAT-21 | **P2 — NEW 2026-09-14 (split out of DAT-3, so a closed P0 cannot imply this became durable)** | **The relay's only subscriber is a `console.log` that cannot throw, so the relay's own failure handling is unreachable in production.** `scripts/outbox-worker.js:66` hardcodes `defaultDeliver` to a single `console.log`; the file's own comment concedes that because this "cannot throw — nothing could ever fail", the attempts/backoff/park paths it is built around are dead code in a real deployment. Consequences: the stdout sink is best-effort with **no replay** (a container recycle or log-rotation race loses the event as far as that sink is concerned), the `schoolos_outbox_parked` gauge can never become non-zero via that path, and `tick()`'s `backoffMs`/`MAX_ATTEMPTS` logic is exercised only by tests that inject a throwing deliver. **Explicitly NOT a delivery loss for webhooks:** `scripts/webhook-worker.js` reads `app.outbox` independently of `processed_at` (HMAC-signed, own backoff, DLQ as `status='failed'`), so a paying school's outbound integrations never depended on the log sink — which is why this is P2 and not the P0 DAT-3 was reframed to be. | `server/scripts/outbox-worker.js:57-69, 106-108` | Give the relay a real sink (ship the batched events to the log pipeline with an ack, or fold the relay into `webhook-worker` and drop the duplicate reader), and make `defaultDeliver` able to fail so the retry/park paths stop being unreachable. Until then, do not read `schoolos_outbox_pending 0` as "events were delivered" — read `schoolos_webhook_deliveries_*` for that. |
| DAT-3 | **P0 — DELIVERY VISIBILITY CLOSED 2026-09-14 (app.js + health-probe.js + alert + tests/dat3-delivery-visibility.test.js, 6/6); the stdout-sink residual below stays OPEN as P2** | **The delivery stage had no gauge, so the one queue signal an operator had reported a drained, healthy queue while every subscriber webhook was failing.** `outbox_pending` counts `processed_at IS NULL`, and the relay sets `processed_at` when it has finished its OWN deliverable — for the log relay, emitting the event to the log sink (stdout, collected by the operator's log pipeline). That is a real delivery to a real sink, so **the column is not lying, and the earlier reading of this row as "`processed_at` is a lie" was wrong.** The webhook fan-out keeps its state in `app.webhook_deliveries` (its own retry and DLQ), and **nothing read it**: not a gauge, not the health probe, not an alert. `webhook_undiscovered` reads `app.outbox.discovered_at` and therefore answers only "did the fan-out SCAN this event" — structurally blind to an event that was scanned, queued for a paying subscriber, and never delivered. | `server/scripts/outbox-worker.js:54-69`; `app.webhook_deliveries` had zero readers | CLOSED (visibility): three gauges — `schoolos_webhook_deliveries_{queued,failed,stalled}` — because collapsing them is what created the blind spot (queued = the fan-out is stuck; failed = a subscriber never got its event; stalled = queued, due and unretried, i.e. the worker is wedged). A new probe check and alert rule `webhook_undelivered` read the same state. The `outbox_pending` HELP text now states what it measures so it cannot be misread again as a delivery count. **The regression is pinned explicitly**: the headline test drains the outbox to a clean `outbox_pending 0` and THEN fails a delivery — the exact state in which the old signal said all was well. Mutation-probed: disabling the gauge emission fails 4 of the 6 tests. **RESIDUAL, deliberately not overclaimed:** `defaultDeliver` is still a `console.log` that cannot throw, so the relay's retry/park paths are unreachable in production and its stdout sink is best-effort with no replay. That is a durability limitation, not a visibility defect — and outbound webhooks do NOT depend on it, because `webhook-worker` reads `app.outbox` independently of `processed_at`. Recorded as its own P2 row (DAT-21) rather than letting a closed P0 imply the log sink became durable. |
| DAT-4 | **P0 — CLOSED 2026-09-13 (migration 076 + tests/outbox-discovery.test.js)** | **Webhook delivery has a 1-hour discovery window, so events older than an outage are silently never delivered.** Discovery is *time*-based (`WHERE o.created_at > now() - interval '1 hour'`), not state-based: a webhook worker down for more than an hour permanently orphans every event in the gap, and because the relay already stamped those rows there is nothing left to re-discover. No gap metric, no alert. | `server/scripts/webhook-worker.js:42` | Persist a high-water-mark cursor and page by `id > cursor ORDER BY id`, or add a `discovered_at` marker plus an idempotent backfill sweep |
