- **Change.** `GET /events/lineage` was gated on a bare `me` while its sibling `GET /events` required ten staff roles, so a student or guardian could read `emitted_by`, `subject` and `correlation_id` for the **entire tenant** — in a feed whose labels embed other families' data (payment and receipt amounts, `PTM booked — <child>`, `Book issued → <student>`, leave requests, `safety.report.filed.v1`). Both doors now share one `EVENT_FEED_ROLES` array (`server/src/routes.js:1331-1339`), so **the drift cannot be reintroduced** without deleting the constant.
- **Regression pin.** `server/tests/security.test.js` → *"API-1: the tenant event lineage feed is not readable by student or parent"*: asserts `403` with no `lineage` key for both roles, and that the principal still gets `200` on **both** `/events` and `/events/lineage` with the response shape unchanged.
- **Note on the tests.** Both new tests **mint their sessions directly** rather than calling `loginAs`. This is not a shortcut: `/auth/login` is limited to 20/min and Track F in the same file *deliberately exhausts that bucket* to prove the spoofed-`X-Forwarded-For` defence. My first attempt used `loginAs` and produced two `429`s — the limiter working correctly. These are authorization tests, so minting the session is the right seam; the same idiom is already used by the RT-7 #5 case above them.
- **Verified.** `TEST_DB_NAME=schoolos_l3_sec node --test tests/security.test.js` → **9 pass, 0 fail** (7 pre-existing + 2 new). No existing assertion was weakened.

### SEC-2 — a non-owner could mint an owner-equivalent role · **CLOSED 2026-09-13**
- **The escalation.** `/roles` authors are `['owner','itadmin']`. `MASK_RE` accepted a wildcard **method** (`*`), `validateMask` only required each pattern to match *some* registered door, and `applyCustomMask` treats an `allow` match as sufficient to **lift** a base-role refusal. So `allow: ['* /*']` validated cleanly — `*` matched every door's method and `/*` prefix-matched every door's path — and an `itadmin` could create that role, assign it, and become owner-equivalent **without breaking any cryptography**. A variation needed no wildcard at all: enumerate any powerful door explicitly.
- **Fix 1 — the wildcard is gone.** `MASK_RE` now accepts only real HTTP verbs (`server/src/routes.js:84`), and a bare root path (`/` or `/*`) is rejected. Verified safe: the shipped Librarian preset uses explicit methods, and **no pattern anywhere in the repo uses a wildcard method**.
- **Fix 2 — the ceiling (the real fix).** Both kernel gate factories now stamp their role list onto the preHandler they return (`routes.js` `require`, `app.js` `requireRole`), and the `onRoute` hook records `door → gate roles` for core **and** plugin doors (`routes.js:73-95`). `validateMask` then refuses any `allow` pattern that matches a door the **author** cannot itself reach, unless the author is `owner`. That is the policy: **the owner is the tenant's authority-granting principal and may grant anything; any other role-admin is capped at its own reach.** No role-admin can create authority it does not hold — by wildcard or by enumeration. `deny` is deliberately exempt, because removing access is never amplification.
- **Amplification is refused honestly.** The check returns a distinct sentinel so the door answers *"A custom role can only grant doors your own role (itadmin) can already reach. Ask the owner to create this role. Nothing was saved."* — rather than reusing the existing "that pattern matches no real screen" copy, which would be false in this case. Nothing is written on refusal (asserted).
- **Regression pin.** `server/tests/custom-roles.test.js` → three tests: the wildcard is refused (`* /*`, `GET /*`, `GET /`, `ALL /fees` all `400`); **itadmin cannot grant `POST /plugins/library/books` (403, and the row is genuinely absent) while the identical owner payload succeeds (200)**; and a deny-only role is still permitted for any author. Verified `TEST_DB_NAME=schoolos_l3_rbac node --test tests/custom-roles.test.js` → **9 pass, 0 fail**, including all six pre-existing custom-role tests — notably *"allow grants a door the base role lacks"*, which is the evidence that the ceiling does not over-block **intended** amplification.
- **Consequence to be aware of.** An `itadmin` can no longer create a role that reaches outside its own authority — including the Librarian role, whose library doors are gated to `owner/principal/coordinator/facilities`. **The owner must create it.** That is the correct authority boundary, and it is why the D-43 acceptance case should be run as the owner.

### SEC-2b (DELETE half) — D-35 violated in the live database, and a landmine re-armed it · **CLOSED 2026-09-13**
- **What was actually wrong, mechanised.** `pg_default_acl` carried a **`postgres`-owned** TABLE default ACL on schema `app`: `{schoolos_app=arwd/postgres}` — the `d` is DELETE — while `schoolos`'s own default ACL was correctly `{schoolos_app=arw/schoolos}`. So every table created **by postgres** silently inherited table-level DELETE for the app role. That is precisely how `app.timetable_periods`, `app.timetable_slots` and `app.timetable_substitutions` came to hold DELETE — three direct D-35 violations in schema `app`. `bootstrap.js:31-32` issues `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE schoolos` only, which repairs the trap for objects created by *schoolos* and cannot outlive the next postgres-authored `CREATE TABLE`. This is now a demonstrated mechanism, not a hypothesis.
- **Fix — new migration `072_acl_repair.sql`.** Re-states the revoke in a **new numbered migration** (immune to re-bootstrap, per the original recommendation) and is **dynamic**, so it repairs drift on any database whose history cannot be reconstructed, not just the 12 tables we happened to observe.
- **Verified on the live drifted dev database, before → after:** 12 tables holding `schoolos_app` DELETE → **1**. Named checks now all `false`: `app.audit_log`, `plugin_razorpay.order_log`, `plugin_razorpay.webhook_log`, `app.timetable_slots`. Re-applying reports *"revoked DELETE on 0 tables"* — idempotent.
- **One deliberate, reasoned exception.** `student.mistake_note` keeps DELETE. A student may hard-delete their **own** notebook note; that door (`student.world/index.js:297`) runs through `withStudentUser`, i.e. **as the app role**, scoped to `creator_user_id` and audited on the hash-chained ledger. Revoking it would break a shipped feature. D-35 is scoped to schema `app` and this is schema `student`. Converting the door to a `SECURITY DEFINER` helper (the `app.purge_staged_rows` precedent in 061) would let the exception go — recorded as a follow-up rather than dropped silently.
- **Dependency analysis before revoking** (revoking a grant a feature needs is its own outage): every `DELETE`/`TRUNCATE` in `server/src` and `server/scripts` was enumerated and matched against the revoked set. The GPS retention purge (`retention-worker.js:81`) runs on the **owner** pool, not the app role, so it is unaffected; `student.mistake_note` was the only app-role delete and was preserved. No revoked grant had a live caller.
- **Regression pin.** `server/tests/acl-d35.test.js` (5 tests, all green). It does not merely read the file — it **simulates the drift first** (`GRANT DELETE … TO schoolos_app`), asserts the violation is observable, then runs the real migration artifact and asserts it is gone, so a no-op migration cannot pass. It then pins D-35 as written (no `app` table grants DELETE), names the money/webhook/audit ledgers explicitly, pins the `student.mistake_note` exception so that revoking it without converting the door is a visible decision, and **warns** about the still-armed default-ACL trap.
- **What is still open, stated plainly.** (1) The `postgres` default ACL **cannot be removed by a migration** — it needs membership in `postgres` or superuser, and `schoolos` is neither (`rolsuper=f`). The exact command is documented in the migration header and warned about by the test; the assert-it-in-CI half of the recommendation guards the **effect** (any table ending up with DELETE) because that is the testable symptom. (2) The **`UPDATE`/`SELECT` half of SEC-2b is NOT closed** — table-level `UPDATE` on `app.users` (holding `password_hash`/`role`), `app.enrollment_history` and `app.files`, and `SELECT` on `app.webhook_subscriptions` (whose `sign_secret` signs every outbound webhook) remain. Narrowing those to column-scoped grants is a separate change with its own blast radius and is tracked.
- **Coop compliance.** Threshold 072 claimed in `.coop/help.md` before writing (071 was the previous max); migration ownership flagged to L1 per D-40 with an offer to hand over or renumber.

### PRD-12 — routes that hard-crash a real tenant · **CLOSED 2026-09-13 (the whole class, not the instances)**
- **Root cause, single mechanism.** `app/src/data/dataset.ts` coerces fixtures to empty objects when `/dataset` omits them (`rec<Person>(data.DEMO_STUDENT, {} as Person)`, likewise `DEMO_GUARDIAN` and `deskSettings`). The declared **non-optional** `Person`/`FrontDeskSettings` shapes are therefore lies in a real tenant, so `.name.split()` / `.languages.join()` / `TERM_DAYS[-1].getTime()` threw *inside render* — past the router, into the root `ErrorBoundary`, replacing the entire app including its sidebar. This is the *same* defect class as §4's success-shaped silence: a type that promises data it does not have.
- **Fixed (9 screens).** The five originally reported — `/parent`, `/parent/chat`, `/student`, `/insights`, `/reception` — plus **four more of the same class found by grepping the whole tree**: `ParentFees.tsx`, `ParentAsk.tsx`, `StudentAsk.tsx` (all three captured the fixture at **module scope**, so demo mode itself would have rendered empty) and **`StudentLearning.tsx:88`**, which rendered a literal **"Grade undefined-undefined"** in a sidebar-linked live route — it did not throw, which is exactly why it survived every previous audit.
- **The fix pattern.** A `DEMO_MODE` split with the guard placed **after the last hook** (so `react-hooks/rules-of-hooks` stays clean), the fixture read moved **inside** the component, and an honest empty state — *"This page is a demo walkthrough …"* — that names where the real records live instead of inventing content. Two shared type predicates (`isLoadedPerson`, `isLoadedStudent`) were added to `demoMode.ts`; no casts, no `any`.
- **Fabrications removed while in these files** (the same class as §4): a hardcoded `'Kavita Sharma'` guardian name that was being written into the **audit trail**, `'Marked at 08:40 by class teacher'` for a register that was never marked, a `'0%'` collection rate and attendance `0%` presented as measured ("all sections nominal"), a fabricated `Cost today ₹11.40`, and `0s` average-answer-time on a desk with no calls. Absent data now renders `—` with plain-language absence.
- **Also removed:** two dead module-scope captures in `state/store.tsx` (`DEMO_STUDENT_ID`/`DEMO_GUARDIAN_ID`, always `undefined`, imported by nothing) — deleted rather than left as a template for the next screen to copy.
- **Verification.** `npx tsc -b` **EXIT 0**; `npx tsc --noEmit --project tsconfig.app.json` **EXIT 0** (note: the repo's root `tsconfig.json` is `files: []` with project references, so the bare `tsc --noEmit` the docs suggest type-checks **nothing** — worth fixing); `npx eslint` on all 11 touched files **0 errors** (one pre-existing `exhaustive-deps` warning, proven not introduced here); `npx vite build` **EXIT 0**, `dist` rebuilt with new chunk hashes and nothing deleted.
- **Render-level proof, not just a compile.** A temporary SSR harness rendered all nine screens against two payloads — an **empty world** (every `/dataset` key blanked, mirroring what the server really returns for a non-demo tenant) and the **real Meridian dataset**. Empty world: all nine render, no literal `undefined`/`NaN` anywhere. Demo world: full demo content preserved on all nine, which also **proved the module-scope bug** — with `DEMO_MODE=true` and a real dataset, `ParentChat`/`StudentHome` had been rendering the *empty* state, i.e. the demo was broken in demo mode too.
- **Class-closure evidence.** A full-tree grep of `app/src` for every fixture reference was dispositioned one by one: guarded, safe, a real-data accessor, or inside a `DEMO_BUILD`-gated demo-only component. `App.tsx`, `components/**`, `plugins/**`, `services/**`: zero. **No unguarded fixture dereference remains.**
- **One residual, named rather than buried:** `ParentFees`' "How the fee is made up" band amounts are hardcoded demo copy. Pre-existing, and now reachable **only** in demo mode because the live branch short-circuits — but it is still a fabricated number on a demo screen and is tracked, not dismissed.

### §5.7 instrument panel — the red suite was TWO stale tests, not eight product failures · **2026-09-13**
- **Measured reality, first-hand.** The full suite is **407 tests across 56 files** — the release docs still claim "114/114 (15 files)" and "183/183", and even this document's earlier text said 392. A full run on the pre-fix tree gave **399 pass / 8 fail**. Every one of those 8 traced to just **two** root causes, neither of which is a product defect:
  1. **One stale regex made SEVEN tests red.** `exams.test.js:56` asserted `/add up to 100%/` against the door's *improved* copy — `"This exam's components must be non-negative numbers that add up to exactly 100%"`. The `409` status assertion **passed**; only the copy regex was stale. Because that test creates the exam that tests 3–7 and 9 then operate on, its failure **cascaded into six further failures that looked like real damage**. The code was right and the test was wrong, so the test moved to `/add up to (exactly )?100%/` — still requiring the copy to explain the 100% rule. **Verified: 9/9 pass** (was 2 pass / 7 fail).
  2. **A calendar bomb that fires EVERY SUNDAY.** `coord-view.test.js` seeds a `timetable_slots` row for "today" via `istDow()`, but the table's CHECK constrains `day_of_week` to Monday–Saturday, so every Sunday the fixture raised `23514 check_violation` and the test failed **deterministically** — it was not flaky, and it was not the product. The scenario (a teacher on approved leave who also has a class *today*) is genuinely not constructible on a non-school day, so it is now an **explicit `t.skip` with the reason**. **Verified: 9 pass / 1 skipped / 0 fail.**
- **Why this matters more than two test fixes.** A suite that is red for reasons unrelated to the product destroys the suite's value as evidence — and this is precisely how "114/114 green" survived in the docs while the only full-run artefact on disk ended `EXIT=1`. The eight reds would have been read as eight product bugs by anyone who did not isolate them. **A failing test must fail for a reason that is true.**
- **Still armed, stated plainly:** the `fees-dunning` bomb (absolute `queued` counts against the seed's September install date) still goes red **2026-10-06**. It needs the same treatment as the Sunday fix — assert relative to a computed school day rather than a hardcoded absolute count. TST-2 is therefore *partially* closed, not closed.
- **A second, smaller trap recorded for the team:** `/tmp` is **not** persisted between harness job invocations. A full-suite log redirected to `/tmp` was silently lost; long-run logs must go to a workspace path. The two runs used here are at `.coop/logs/`.

### §4 H5 / OPS-4 / TST-1 — the relay no longer mistakes failure for success · **2026-09-13**
- **The defect, restated precisely.** The park path stamped `processed_at = now()`, and the only queue gauge is `count(*) WHERE processed_at IS NULL`. So the instant a poison row exhausted `MAX_ATTEMPTS`, **the pending gauge FELL**. A total relay outage — every event dropped — was metrically *identical to a healthy idle system*. Operationally this is the worst shape a defect can take: the one signal an on-call engineer watches moves the wrong way, and reassuringly.
- **`migrations/073_outbox_parked_at.sql`** adds `parked_at` so three states are permanently distinct: **delivered** (`processed_at` set), **parked** (`parked_at` set, `processed_at` **NULL**), **pending** (neither). Parking deliberately does **not** set `processed_at`: such a row is terminal for the relay but was never delivered, so absorbing it into the "done" population is the lie being removed.
- **The gauge now reports failure positively.** `schoolos_outbox_pending` excludes parked rows, and a NEW `schoolos_outbox_parked` counts them — non-zero means undelivered events. It is omitted entirely on a pre-073 database rather than reported as `0`, because a fabricated "no failures" reading is the same defect class.
- **Two follow-on traps I had to close or the fix would have leaked:** (1) `retention-worker.js` aged out only `processed_at`, so parked rows — which now have none — would have accumulated **forever**; both terminal states now age out on their own timestamp. (2) Rows parked by the *old* worker still carried `processed_at`, so 073 **reclassifies** them (keys on the surviving `last_error` marker) and clears the false `processed_at` — otherwise the fix would apply to future failures only.
- **The component is now testable at all.** The file ran its poll loop at *module scope*, so `import` started a second relay; and its only subscriber was `console.log`, which cannot throw. Between those two facts, **backoff and parking were unreachable from any test** — which is precisely why this bug survived. The loop now runs only as the process entry point, and the subscriber is injectable (`tick({ deliver })`). `tests/outbox-relay.test.js` (**8/8**) pins delivery, retry-with-backoff, parking, the three-state model, the `/metrics` gauge, and the 073 backfill **by executing the real migration artifact** against a synthetic legacy row.
- **Regression probe (the assertion is real, not decorative).** Reintroducing `processed_at = now()` on the park path made exactly the intended tests fail — *"a parked row is NOT processed — it was never delivered"* and *"no row is ever both parked and processed"* (5 pass / 2 fail); restoring the fix returned 8/8. The pin fails for the right reason.
- Regression sweep over every touched area (retention/day-worker, API keys + webhooks, gateway/workflow, admissions): **21/21 pass**.

### §5.1 SEC-1 / §3 — RLS became a database boundary, not a call-site convention · **2026-09-13**
- **Verified before touching anything**: the API pool authenticated as `schoolos`, which OWNS every table and carries `rolbypassrls = true` (`schoolos`: rolsuper=f, **rolbypassrls=t**; `schoolos_app`: both false). For the owner, RLS is not merely unenforced — it is SKIPPED. Only the 238 `withUser(...)` sites were protected (they explicitly `SET LOCAL ROLE schoolos_app`); every bare `q(...)` site ran as the owner. **Tenant isolation was therefore a property of call-site discipline, not of the database** — and the codebase shipped 74+ bare sites, all of which were one careless edit away from a cross-tenant read.
- **The default is inverted.** `q()` now runs inside a transaction with `SET LOCAL ROLE schoolos_app`, so RLS applies to every ordinary query whether or not its author thought about tenancy. `sysq()` is the new explicitly-named owner helper; `tx()` pins `SET LOCAL ROLE schoolos`. `SET LOCAL` is transaction-scoped, so a downgrade can never leak onto a pooled connection another request later borrows.
- **Fail-closed, demonstrated.** With no `schoolos.tenant_id` GUC the policy evaluates `tenant_id = NULL` → **0 rows**. A missed call site now leaks *nothing* instead of leaking every school. Its cost is stated rather than hidden: an un-migrated site returns an empty list instead of an error, which is its own flavour of success-shaped silence — so the boundary test asserts a real tenant door (`/students`) still returns real rows.
- **103 owner-privileged sites made explicit and auditable** across 32 files (62 app.js, 4 routes.js, 1 onboarding.js, the plugin tree via `kernel`). Plugin modules now receive BOTH `q` (safe) and `sysq` (privileged), so **the name means the same thing everywhere** — previously `q` was safe in `app.js` but privileged inside plugins, a footgun that would have manufactured the next P0.
- **Fourteen self-inflicted breakages caught by scanning for them**, which is the part worth institutionalising: renaming kernel destructures left dangling `q` bindings (3 × `bindKernel(sysq){ kernelQ = q; }`, 9 × `makeLifecycleGate(q, ID)`), and worst, `bindEmailKernel(q)` was handed the now-SAFE helper while it reads `app.plugins` on the owner path — that would have **silently broken password-reset email**, a failure that looks like an SMTP problem, not a privilege change. A near-miss on the same theme: the rename regex skipped `q` preceded by `.`, so **`kernel.q(...)` was never renamed**; because the kernel now hands out both helpers, those 5 sites silently flipped from owner to constrained and began returning nothing (500s in `pack.dpdp`/`student.world`), diagnosed from three suite failures with no permission or timeout errors in the log.
- **`tests/rls-boundary.test.js` — NEW, 8/8.** Pins that `q()`'s effective role is `schoolos_app`; that `schoolos_app` carries neither BYPASSRLS nor superuser; that a GUC-less tenant read returns 0 while `sysq` still sees the real population (proving the 0 is RLS and not an empty table); `withUser` scoping including a non-existent tenant seeing ZERO; a real tenant door returning real rows; **that no bare `q(` exists anywhere in `src/`** (so every site declares its privilege); that the privileged count is PINNED at 103 so privilege cannot grow silently; and that every raw `pool.connect` outside `db.js` is role-constrained or allowlisted with a stated reason.
- **Regression probe**: reverting `q()` to `pool.query` produced **6 pass / 2 fail**, with the effective role reading `schoolos` and a tenant read returning **14 rows instead of 0** — i.e. the cross-tenant exposure itself.
- **Two suite blockers diagnosed to their true roots, one of them mine.** (1) `onboard.test.js` used the now-constrained `q()` to inspect `app.tenants`/`app.users` — a privileged test-side read, so it now says `sysq` (only ONE file imported `q`; every other file uses the raw pool for fixtures). (2) `scripts/seed.js` used `Math.random()` for attendance, so the SAME student measured **70% / 80% / 90%** across three fresh seeds; with the exams eligibility gate enforcing a **75% floor**, a 70% draw failed `exam-depth.test.js` at publish and cancelled all 8 tests — **pre-existing flakiness that passed earlier runs by luck**. All 5 uses now run through a seeded mulberry32 `rand()`; verified two fresh seeds give identical attendance (90%), and exam-depth is 8/8.
- **Full suite after all of it: 423 tests, 422 pass, 0 fail, 0 cancelled, 1 explicit skip, EXIT=0.**
- **Residual, stated plainly:** an empty-string (not NULL) `schoolos.tenant_id` on a connection makes every RLS policy raise `22P02` instead of denying. I proved `withUser('')` reproduces it exactly, and that `withUser` is never called with a falsy tenant, that no `set_config` in `src/` is session-scoped, and that `pg_db_role_setting` holds no `schoolos.*` default — **so the source of the empty GUC remains unexplained** and is carried forward. The Phase 5 hardening is to rewrite policy predicates as `app.current_tenant()` = `NULLIF(current_setting('schoolos.tenant_id', true), '')::uuid`, so a policy can never raise on connection state.

### §8 residual CLOSED via migration 074 — a policy must DENY, never RAISE · **2026-09-13**
- **The residual from SEC-1, closed.** An empty-string (not NULL) `schoolos.tenant_id` made 94 policy predicates cast `''::uuid` → **22P02**, turning an authorization rule into a 500 for the whole request. Investigating it revealed a second, larger hole in the same code: `current_setting('schoolos.tenant_id')` **without** the missing_ok argument raises **42704 "unrecognized configuration parameter"** whenever the GUC was never set in the session — a latent hard error on 12 tables. Quantified: **34 predicates carried the 42704 hole, 60 the 22P02 hole.**
- **The rule this establishes:** an authorization predicate has exactly two acceptable outcomes — the row is visible, or it is not. It must never be able to FAIL the query. Before 074 the same "no tenant context" state *denied* on most tables and *errored* on others; uniformity is what makes the boundary reason-able at all. Every predicate now routes through `app.current_tenant()` / `app.current_user_id()`, which normalise NULL **and** `''` to NULL → UNKNOWN → deny.
- **Live-verified, all three directions:** empty-string GUC → **0 rows** (previously 22P02); unset GUC on a previously-42704 table → **0 rows**; a real tenant → **375 students** (proving the fix denies bad context without denying good context).
- **The helpers return TEXT, not uuid — deliberately.** My first attempt returned uuid and the migration died with `operator does not exist: text = uuid`, because some predicates compare `tenant_id::text = current_setting(...)`. Substituting an expression of the **same type** as the one replaced keeps every existing cast and comparison meaning exactly what it meant, so the sole behavioural change is the closed hole.
- **My own migration was buggy first, in precisely the class this document is about.** It looped over `pg_policy` and issued `ALTER POLICY` inside that loop — mutating the catalogue rows being scanned, so PostgreSQL **skipped rows**: it silently hardened 44 of 94 predicates and reported success. The tell was a discrepancy between two databases (one freshly migrated showed 50 remaining; one migrated in two passes showed 0). Fixed by materialising the candidate list into a temp table first, plus an explicit `RAISE EXCEPTION` when candidates ≠ rewritten, so a partial hardening can never again report success.
- **THE BIGGER FINDING — `scripts/seed.js` replays 35 migration files at runtime, and that replay UNDOES policy hardening.** Measured step by step on a single database: `after migrate → inline=0 · after bootstrap → inline=0 · after seed → inline=50`. The replay re-creates the policies from migrations 009–048, and those files write predicates with inline `current_setting`. **So 074 could never have held in any seeded environment — that is, in every test run and every demo.** Worse, `migrate.js` refuses to run once a recorded migration's checksum drifts, so the old migrations **cannot** be corrected at source. The fix is that the hardening is the last writer: the seed re-applies 074 after the replay (it is idempotent), with the rule written into the file — anything that hardens, replaces or constrains a policy created by those migrations must be re-applied there too, or the seed silently reverts it on every run and the regression shows up only in seeded environments.
- **Regression pin**: `tests/rls-policy-hardening.test.js` (6/6) asserts that a policy inlining `current_setting` cannot reappear **anywhere in the catalogue**, so both the seed-replay revert and any newly hand-written policy of the old shape fail the suite. Verified the pin bites: removing the seed block took it from 6/6 to 4/6.
- **Full suite after 074 + both seed changes: 429 tests, 428 pass, 0 fail, 0 cancelled, 1 explicit skip, EXIT=0.**

### §8 — PRD-13 (part 2) closed: the pickup OTP is finally sent — and fails honestly when it cannot be · **2026-09-13**
`ops.visitors` minted the pickup code with the kernel OTP pattern (CSPRNG → scrypt+salt →
`plugin_visitors.pickup_otp`, whose RLS denies the app role) and then handed it to **nobody**: no rail
call, no `app.communications` row, not even a queue entry. The emitted event nonetheless read *"pickup
requested — OTP sent to primary guardian"* and the audit read *"OTP issued to primary guardian"*, so the
front desk believed a code was on its way while the guardian had nothing to read out. Release is
fail-closed, so the practical effect was that **every pickup silently degraded to the photo-ID override
while the record claimed the identity check had happened** — the worst place in a school product for the
ledger to lie.

**Two defects, not one.** Fixing the send exposed the second: the rails REFUSE anything that is not strict
E.164, and phone numbers are stored the way a school office types them — the seed itself keeps
`+91 9720000243`, with a space. A number can look entirely plausible on every screen and still be
undeliverable, so handing the stored value to the rail failed outright. `pack.dpdp` and
`admissions.funnel` each held an identical private normaliser and `scripts/comms-dispatcher.js` a third,
weaker one (a bare 10-digit audience entry became `+9876500099` — a wrong-country number); that rule now
lives once, in `src/plugins/_shared/phone.js` (`toE164` → E.164, or `null` when nothing usable exists).
Validate-only is not enough: doors must normalise.

**The fix.** The code is handed to `rails.sms` **directly** — never queued, because a live single-use code
must not sit in `app.communications`, which six roles read with `SELECT *`; the ledger gets a REDACTED
row as evidence (the SEC-0 pattern the reset door already uses). `bindKernel(sysq)` is called first,
because `rails.*` are absent from the plugin catalog so their `register()` never runs in the API process
and an unbound rail does not fail — `readSettings` throws, the catch "degrades to logger", and every send
returns `{ok:true, provider:'logger'}` whatever the tenant configured. The same carrier rule the
dispatcher and the reset door use decides the outcome; this is not a third definition.

**Compensation when it cannot be delivered.** The vault row is withdrawn, the visit returns to its
previous status with `otp_request_id = NULL`, an audit row explains why, and the door answers 503
pointing at photo ID. A live code nobody holds is worse than none: it left the visit `pickup_pending`
with a dangling `otp_request_id`, so `/pickup/verify` answered "No pickup code is active for this visit"
at a desk that had just been told to expect one. Failing loudly sends the desk to the audited override
instead — the truth about what is actually being relied on. Wording now names the carrier and labels a
sandbox send as "(sandbox — no carrier configured)"; the outbox payload contract is unchanged.

`tests/pickup-otp-delivery.test.js` (**5/5**) pins: an evidence row addressed to the guardian in sendable
E.164 (asserting the normalised form AND that it differs from the stored one, so the broken form cannot be
re-pinned); the raw code absent from the ledger while present in the SMS body (non-vacuous SEC-0); no
`delivered_at` for a sandbox carrier; the event/audit naming the carrier; and the compensation path
exercised **for real** through a deterministic rail misconfiguration (no stubbing, no NODE_ENV games).
**All three mutations reproduce the defect**: dropping normalisation → the rail refuses; dropping
compensation → the door returns `200 pickup_pending` with a code while nothing was sent; dropping the
evidence row → "there was none before this fix". Note the label assertion had to target
`app.events.label`: `emitEvent` only folds the label into the outbox payload when the caller passes none,
so asserting it from the outbox would have been an assertion that could never fail.

**Still open in PRD-13:** the dispatcher resolves only the FIRST usable recipient, so a 300-guardian
notice reaches one person and the row still reads `sent`; and `day-worker`.

### §8 — three credential rails and one door that stopped lying (PRD-4 / PLG-3 / OPS-5) · **2026-09-13**
The rails were ghosts. `rails.email`, `rails.sms` and `rails.whatsapp` ship with full manifests, settings
contracts and working send functions, and they are declared in `app.capability_contracts` (045) — but they had
**no row in `app.plugins` for any tenant**. Three consequences, all reachable by a customer:
the plugin manager listed 23 of the 26 shipped plugins and the rails could not be seen, switched on or
configured; `POST /plugins/rails.sms/settings` answered 404; and `/me/reset-request` gates on *"does this
tenant have a rails.email row"* — a question whose answer was always no, so **self-service password reset was
503 for every tenant, forever**, and the same absence quietly pinned the OTP and comms dispatchers to the
logger provider.

`migrations/078_register_credential_rails.sql` seeds the three rows for tenants that already exist, and
`plugin-catalog.js` carries them for every tenant created afterwards (`ensurePluginCatalog` at signup and the
self-heal in `GET /plugins`) — the same two-part mechanism the other 26 plugins use. They arrive
**`registered`, switched off**: a rail is a credential, so switching one on stays an audited owner action.
That also forced a second, sharper fix: with a row now existing for every school, the reset door's original
"a row exists" gate would have become decorative, so both channel gates require **`status='enabled'`** — the
owner's deliberate act of configuring a channel — and say so in the 503 copy instead of implying the platform
tried to send.

Then the door that was success-shaped. `/auth/otp/request` answered `{sent:true}` and minted a code into the
vault **while sending nothing anywhere**: the code left the process only through a log line or the opt-in dev
echo. It now resolves the channel first, and the ordering is deliberate — the channel gate runs **before**
anything is minted, because the `ops.visitors` bug in this same lane showed what a refusal placed after a
vault write costs (a live one-time credential for a message that was never sent). The answer describes what
happened: `{sent:true, channel:'sms', provider:'logger', sandbox:true}` when the rail took it,
`{channel:'dev-echo', sandbox:true}` when the development echo is the delivery, and a **503/502 with
"nothing was sent"** when it could not be delivered — in which case the minted row is withdrawn and the audit
row says `WITHDRAWN`, not `issued`, so the ledger can never claim a credential was delivered to a phone that
never received one.

**Verified live, on the running service** (not only in tests): with the SMS channel off, the OTP door answers
503 *"This school has no SMS channel switched on yet…"* — the old answer was `{sent:true}`; the owner switches
`rails.sms` on through the plugin door; the same request then answers
`{sent:true, channel:"sms", provider:"logger", sandbox:true}`. Same for reset: 503 with actionable copy, one
owner click, then 200. The plugin manager now lists **29** plugins with the rails in their real states
(`rails.whatsapp` still registered).

**Tests**: `server/tests/rails-catalog-otp.test.js` **7/7**, written so the defect fails them — the self-heal
is asserted against a tenant with the rows deleted first, the enable path goes through the plugin door, the
OTP assertions check delivery *evidence* (carrier name, sandbox flag, exactly one live vault row) rather than
the status code alone, the failing-send case points an `http-generic` provider at a dead port (no mocking) and
asserts the vault is empty and the ledger says `WITHDRAWN` afterwards, and the production half is asserted
against the same `delivery-honesty` decision function the dispatcher uses so the rule keeps one definition.

**Honest limits**: the channel gates remain **existence-dependent** — an unknown phone still gets
`{sent:true}` while a known account in a school with no channel gets 503 — exactly the tradeoff the reset door
already documents (the alternative, a uniform 200, silently strands the user; the login door already answers
409 `multiTenant` for known identities). The five unscheduled workers in the PRD-4 row were never part of this
fix and remain **OPS-8**.

### §8 — the client half: a librarian can finally SEE the module (RBAC-3) · **2026-09-13**
Fixing enforcement (§8 RBAC-1) made every library DOOR authorise the librarian, and the account still looked
broken in the browser, because the screen decided from the wrong fact. `LibraryLive.tsx` computed
`isStaff = ['owner','principal','coordinator','facilities'].includes(live.role)`, and a custom role
deliberately reports its **base** role — `/me` answered `role:'teacher'`. So the librarian saw the module title
with none of its controls, which is precisely what the register meant by "it fails at both layers".

The client needed one thing it did not have: the caller's own mask. `/me` now returns `role_key` and
`custom: {allow, deny}` — **the caller's own permissions, never anyone else's**, and still only a rendering
hint: every door re-decides on the server. A new `app/src/lib/grants.ts` re-implements the matcher as a
*display mirror* (`roleOrGrant(session, builtinRoles, doors)`), `LibraryLive` asks it, and the built-in list
remains the fallback so nothing changes for the twelve shipped roles. `completeSignIn` now re-reads `/me`
after login, because the login response carries only `{name, role, email}` — without that a fresh sign-in
rendered the module without controls until a manual reload.

**Two permission checks that lived INSIDE handlers were also lifted**, because a `require()` gate cannot lift
a refusal it does not make. `routes.js` admitted the gap in a comment ("KNOWN LIMITATION (tracked as RBAC-1,
Phase 2): a custom role granted an explicit `GET /fees/obligations` mask is refused here rather than
honoured"). A new `canReach(req, roles)` = built-in role **or** an explicit mask grant for this exact request,
backed by the decision `authenticate` already memoises (no extra query), is now used at the fees ledger, both
staff-case-log refusals, the file-download admin check and the safeguarding authority check. **Scope and
segregation-of-duties comparisons were deliberately left alone** — a mask grant answers "may you reach this
door", never "is this row yours" or "may you also be the checker" — and the remainder is recorded as
**RBAC-1b** with a count and the judgement each site needs, rather than being quietly lifted or quietly
dropped.

**Verified against the live service, not only in tests** (backend restarted on the new code): the owner
created the shipped preset; `librarian@meridian.edu` was created as a real separate account through the
people console and assigned the role; `/me` returns `role_key: librarian` with 6 grants; the catalog, loans
and overdue doors answer 200, a book was added through the door, finance answers 403, and a **plain teacher
still gets `role teacher not permitted`** on the same door — the grant is specific, not a blanket opening.
The 409 that appeared first was the lifecycle gate telling the truth ("Library is switched off") for a tenant
that had the plugin registered but not enabled; the owner enabled it and the doors opened, which is the flow
a customer actually walks.

**Tests**: `server/tests/rbac-plugin-edge.test.js` **6/6** (D-43 librarian end-to-end on a separate account,
the in-handler grant lift including a control door the mask does not name, `/me` scoping, deny on a plugin
door, deny on an ungated door, and a non-granted user untouched). `server/tests/frontend-grants.test.js`
**7/7** — the first real frontend test in the project: Node 26 strips TypeScript types natively, so the client
module is imported and asserted *against the server's own matcher across the same table of cases*, which is
what stops the two copies from drifting; plus a source assertion that the screen asks the mask. The web app
still has no DOM runner, so the visual half is verified by the live sign-in above, not by a test.

**Also found and corrected while here**: register row **SEC-5** was stale. Verified live — a student and a
technician get 403 on `/fees/obligations` (no tenant-wide leak) and a parent gets 200 with
`scoped: own_children`. The row claimed the fallback returned up to 500 tenant obligations to any role.

### §8 — RBAC-1 closed: the librarian account the owner asked for now works · **2026-09-13**
The owner's original complaint was that "the library module is in principal login whereas it should be as a
plugin and added with a separate librarian account". The library IS a plugin, and there IS a shipped custom-role
preset for exactly this (`app/src/screens/RolesAdmin.tsx` LIBRARIAN_START, the D-43 acceptance quick start,
`base_role: 'teacher'` + six explicit library allow patterns). The preset never worked, for a reason that had
nothing to do with the preset: **mask enforcement lived entirely inside `registerRoutes`.**

Only the kernel's `require()`/`me` doors called `applyCustomMask`. Every plugin door is gated by a *different*
gate — `src/app.js` `requireRole`, handed to `loadPlugins` — which checked `roles.includes(req.user.role)` and
nothing else. So both directions were wrong, and the second one is the bug the owner hit:

* a matched **DENY** was silently ignored on plugin doors. The owner restricts a role, is told it saved, and the
  plugin door still answers 200 — worse than not offering the feature, because the restriction is believed;
* a matched **ALLOW** could not lift a plugin door's role refusal, and `library`'s STAFF is
  `['owner','principal','coordinator','facilities']` — **`teacher` is not in it**. The teacher-based librarian
  therefore got `403 role teacher not permitted` on every library door. The role existed, the mask was stored,
  and the feature did not work. (There is no `librarian` builtin role, and there does not need to be: the
  product's own design for this is a custom role, and the D-43 criterion is that the custom role works.)

**The fix is one shared implementation, not a second one.** The store, the matcher (`patternMatches`,
`maskDecides`) and the decision rule moved out of `routes.js` into `src/custom-roles.js`; `routes.js` keeps only
the half that needs the live route registry (pattern legality and the amplification ceiling, `validateMask`).
Both gates now call the same `applyMask` with the same resolution order (deny → allow → base role), and
enforcement **also rides `authenticate`**, so a DENY is honoured even on doors that carry no role gate at all
(`/dataset` is gated by `authenticate` alone; before this a deny naming it was unenforceable).

**A real bug found by the test, in the fix itself:** the first version constructed the module twice — once in
`app.js`, once in `routes.js` — and each instance kept its **own** binding cache. The assign door cleared the
kernel instance's cache while the plugin gate kept answering from its own copy for up to 30s, so a newly added
DENY did not take effect on plugin doors. There is now exactly one instance (`export const customRoles`), which
is the point: the two gates must see the same binding. Found because the deny test asserted the *reason* in the
refusal body and not merely the status.

`tests/rbac-plugin-edge.test.js` (**4/4**) pins the whole acceptance path: the owner creates the shipped preset;
a **real separate account** (`librarian.accept@meridian.edu`) is created through the people.admin door with the
teacher base and assigned the role; that account can then read the catalog, add a book and list loans — while a
plain teacher gets `403 not permitted` on the same door — **and finance refuses it**, which is the other half of
the D-43 criterion. Plus: a DENY is honoured on a plugin door (proved non-vacuous by first asserting that the
`s facilities` base role reaches that door without any custom role), a DENY is honoured on an ungated door, and
a user without a role_key is untouched.

**Mutations, bracketed by green baselines** (an earlier attempt produced void results — the harness itself was
failing to create its database under load, so every run must be bracketed by a passing baseline to be
attributable): restoring the old plugin gate fails **D-43**; dropping the deny at its computation point fails
the **ungated-door** test. The plugin-door deny test survives *either* single mutation on purpose — the deny is
enforced twice (once in `authenticate`, once by the gate reading the memoised decision), so it is defence in
depth rather than two paths that can drift.

The shell's former hardcoded per-role switch is now a demo-only fallback; live navigation and route guards use
the server's effective-access catalog (FE-2/RBAC-6). The separate RBAC-4 finding still stands: library *write*
is granted to `facilities`.

### §8 — PRD-17 closed: the approvals inbox fabricated a parent notice, and PRD-13's fan-out with it · **2026-09-13**
The decide executor's `kind:'message'` branch inserted the comms row directly as `status='delivered'` with
`sent_at` **and** `delivered_at` = `now()`, emitted *"Parent notice SENT — guardians (…, approved by …)"*, and
returned `execution.status='delivered'`. Nothing was sent: no rail call, no queue entry, no dispatcher pass.
Nothing *could* be sent either — the dispatcher only claims `status='queued'`, so a row born `delivered` is
unreachable for delivery for the rest of its life. The audience written was `[p.audience ?? 'guardians']`, a
free-text **label**, which no dispatcher can resolve a recipient from (`'guardians'`, `'Route 7 guardians'`).
`delivered` is the strongest claim the schema makes — `sent` and `delivered` are separate values and the
dispatcher itself only ever sets `sent_at` — so approving a fee circular told the approver, the timeline, the
notices list and the action record that every guardian had it. `comms.whatsapp` renders `delivered_at` to
users, so the lie was on screen.

**Both halves had to be fixed together, or neither.** Queueing multi-recipient rows is only honest once the
dispatcher stops sending to one address, and that fan-out is the other half of PRD-13's register row:

**Fan-out (`scripts/comms-dispatcher.js`).** `recipientOf` returned only the FIRST usable recipient and the
row was then marked `sent`, so a notice queued for 300 guardians reached exactly one person. The audience
was always a list; treating it as a single address was the fabrication. `recipientsOf`/`recipientEmailsOf`
now send to **every** recipient, and the persisted receipt states the count — `provider_ref` ends
`(75/75 recipients)`, so a reader never has to guess. **Partial is not success**: if any recipient fails the
result is `ok:false` with `k of n delivered; first failure: …`, so the row lands in the retry/park path and
its `last_error` says so — a broadcast that half-arrived must not read `sent` on any screen. The per-row cap
(`COMMS_MAX_RECIPIENTS`, default 500) **refuses rather than truncates**: silently dropping the tail and then
reporting "50/50 recipients" would be the same lie in a new place.

**Execution (`src/routes.js`).** Recipients are resolved from **machine-readable identifiers** —
`recipients[]`, `studentIds[]`/`studentId`, or `routeCode` against `students.bus_route` — through a new
exported `resolveNoticeRecipients`, then queued as `status='queued'` for the dispatcher to deliver and judge
honestly. Raise sites now carry what they always had in scope: `transport.gps` and the legacy transport
raiser pass `routeCode` (whose payload `detail` already *claimed* "Audience resolved from the roster" while
resolving nothing), and `health.clinic` passes `studentId`.
**A label is not a selector, deliberately**: the bare word `'guardians'` resolves to **nobody** rather than
to every guardian in the school, because a clinic notice reads "was sent home" and tenant-wide fan-out would
tell every parent about one child.

**A notice that cannot be addressed is recorded as unsent, not as delivered** — no comms row at all (a row
is delivery *evidence*, and there is no delivery to evidence), `execution.status='unaddressed'`, a `warn`
event that says NOT sent, and an audit outcome naming it. The approval still returns 200: the decision was
the approver's to make, and a 4xx would leave the action open with no way to explain why.

`tests/notice-execution.test.js` (**5/5**) pins: approving queues with resolved E.164 recipients and no
`delivered`/timestamps; **one dispatcher pass reaches all 75 of a route's guardians** and still does not
claim `delivered_at` under a sandbox carrier; an unaddressable notice writes **no row at all**; the clinic
notice resolves *that child's* guardians only; and the resolver returns empty for every pure-label payload
while normalising explicit numbers. **Both mutations reproduce the defect** — restoring `'delivered'` fails
"nothing has been delivered yet — no carrier has seen this row", and queueing the label fails "a route notice
addresses the whole roster, got `["Route Route 7 guardians"]`" (which also exposed a cosmetic double-"Route"
in that label, now fixed).

**A second test pinned the defect and was corrected**: `tests/hr-clinic.test.js` asserted
`status === 'delivered'` with the comment *"comms rail executed the notice"* — while no rail ran. It now
asserts the queued row, its NULL timestamps and its real E.164 audience. That is a strengthening: the
assertion can now fail, and it could not before.

**Independent verification** (separate agent, read-only, its own database) confirmed the reading of
`routes.js:1076-1081`, found the same defect shape in `scripts/seed.js:284-286` (demo rows hard-coded
`'delivered'` with hand-written timestamps, guarded to non-production — recorded, not yet fixed), hardened
`isSandboxCarrier` (a `' logger '` provider used to read as a real carrier), and found an **uncompensated
early return in the pickup fix itself**: it normalised the phone AFTER the vault write, so the 409 left the
visit `pickup_pending` with a live `otp_request_id` and an unconsumed vault row. Normalisation now happens
before anything is written, with a test asserting the ordering.

### §8 — PRD-13 (part 3) closed: the daily dunning step never ran — it imported a module that does not exist · **2026-09-13**
`scripts/day-worker.js` step 4 did `await import('../src/plugins/money.invoicing/dunning.js')` and called
`runDunning()` on it. **That path does not exist** (confirmed on disk), so the import threw
MODULE_NOT_FOUND on *every* run, the catch logged `day.dunning { status: 'skipped' }`, and the worker
reported a clean day. Overdue-fee reminders — a money-collecting path — never ran on schedule, while the
HTTP door `POST /fees/dunning/run` worked, so the feature looked healthy from every screen.

The worker's comment framed this as transitional ("until L1's module lands the step logs `skipped`, never
crashes"), and a test pinned it as correct:
`assert.equal(dunning.status, 'skipped', 'L1 dunning module absent → contained skip, no crash')`
(`tests/l2-retention-dayworker.test.js`). But the module was never going to land at that path, because
**the sweep already existed** — as `scripts/dunning-reminders.js`, a self-executing CLI script with **no
exports at all**, so the hook's `mod.runDunning` contract could not have matched it even after "landing".
An assertion that a component is absent is not a containment test; it is a defect with a green tick.

**The fix.** `scripts/dunning-reminders.js` now exports `runDunning({ q, quiet })` with the CLI entry kept
behind the same double-duty guard `day-worker.js` uses, so the daily worker and the cron entry run the SAME
sweep instead of two divergent ideas of one. `quiet` is a deliberate tri-state — `null` decides from the
clock per tenant (the real cron behaviour), `true` reports quiet and queues nothing, `false` sweeps
regardless — because a sweep whose only honest answer is "it depends what time it is" cannot be tested, and
IST quiet hours are 21:00–08:00, so an unparameterised test would pass at 3pm and fail at 3am.

**The corrected assertion is a strengthening, not a weakening.** What that test actually cared about is
containment ("started, did not crash the day"), so it now asserts that on *both* paths: the step performs a
real sweep (`status: 'ok'`, a numeric `queued`), and a sweep that throws is still reported rather than
crashing the cron. The failure branch is now exercised by making the sweep fail instead of by asserting
that its module is missing — which is a test that could only ever pass while the feature was broken.

`tests/day-worker-dunning.test.js` (**6/6**) pins: no `import` of the dead path remains while the comment
documenting it is allowed to name it; the step reports work rather than a skip; containment on a synthetic
failure; quiet hours reported as quiet and not as failure; the standalone CLI still exits 0 on a clean
sweep (documented contract: 1 when reminders were queued); and every step the composed day depends on is
still exported. **Mutation-verified**: restoring the dead import path fails both "real sweep" assertions
while the containment test still passes, which is the correct discrimination.

**Still open in PRD-13:** the dispatcher resolves only the FIRST usable recipient, so a 300-guardian notice
reaches one and the row still reads `sent`.

### §8 — PRD-13 (part 1) closed: a sandbox carrier is no longer recorded as a delivery · **2026-09-13**
The mechanism, established by reading the rails rather than the register: `rails.sms`/`rails.email`/
`rails.whatsapp` all default `provider` to `'logger'` when a tenant has no credentials — **and also when
the per-tenant settings READ FAILS** ("degrading to logger provider"), so a transient database hiccup
demotes a real carrier to a sandbox one. A logger provider then returns `{ ok: true }`, because writing
a log line *did* succeed. Three call sites treated that as delivery:
- **`comms-dispatcher.js`** stamped `status='sent', sent_at=now(), last_error=NULL`. With `last_error`
  deliberately cleared, `provider='logger'` was the only surviving trace — next to a status every
  consumer, the UI and any audit reads as "sent". SEC-0 had already written the same realisation down
  for the reset door: *"sandbox is the permanent default for any tenant without SMTP credentials … so
  this was never a developer-only state."*
- **`app.js` reset door**, which sends directly (it needs the raw code in the message, kept out of the
  ledger by SEC-0) and so bypassed the dispatcher entirely — and stamped **`delivered_at=now()`**
  unconditionally. That is a *stronger* false claim than `sent`: the schema keeps `sent` and `delivered`
  apart, the dispatcher only ever sets `sent_at`, and `comms.whatsapp` renders `delivered_at` to users.
  This one was found while fixing the others, not in the register.
- **`dispatch`'s `log-only` branch** (unsupported channel, or no resolvable recipient) returned
  `ok: true, provider: 'log-only'` by construction, and its comment claimed "the reason is on the
  record" — it was not, because the caller clears `last_error` on success.

**What the fix is, and deliberately is not.** A log line is a legitimate carrier in a sandbox and a lie
in production, so that single distinction now lives in one place — `src/plugins/_shared/delivery-honesty.js`
(`isSandboxCarrier`, `isSandboxResult`, `sandboxAllowed`) — used by the dispatcher **and** by the doors
that send directly and write their own row, so none of them can invent their own version. In production a
sandbox result is terminal and `outcome: 'undeliverable'`, never `sent`, with the reason in `last_error`;
`sent_at` stays NULL; `delivered_at` is only ever stamped for a **real** carrier (`sandbox: false`, which
`rails.email` reports explicitly, or a non-sandbox provider name, which is all `rails.sms` offers). The
sandbox path itself is **unchanged** — the sandbox tests pin the logger carrier as deliberate and still
assert `outcome: 'sent'`, so the fix is scoped to where the lie caused harm instead of breaking a design
decision to prove a point. `sandboxAllowed(env)` is injectable precisely because `config.env` is read at
import time: a rule that cannot be tested is a rule that will rot.

**The lie is now measurable, not merely absent.** `/metrics` gains `schoolos_comms_sandbox_sent` — rows
whose status is `sent` but whose carrier reached nobody — and `schoolos_comms_failed`. Previously
`comms_queued` *fell* as those rows drained, so a school with no provider watched a clean, emptying
queue: the failure signature was identical to health. (Same defect class as 073's parked rows.)

`tests/comms-honesty.test.js` (**7/7**) pins: production → `undeliverable`, `status='failed'`,
`sent_at IS NULL`, reason naming the consequence; the same row in a sandbox → still `sent` (the
non-vacuous half: the gate discriminates by runtime, it does not just fail every logger send); a real
carrier (`smtp` `sandbox:false`, `http-generic`, `cloud-api`, `twilio`) is never downgraded in either
mode; and the reset door writes `provider='logger'` with `delivered_at IS NULL`. **Both gates verified by
mutation** — disabling either makes the specific assertion fail with the old output
(`outcome: "sent"` / "delivered_at must stay NULL"). All 29 tests across `l2-rails-live`,
`l2-rails-email-reset`, `rails`, `l2-retention-dayworker` and the dispatcher pass unchanged, which is the
evidence that sandbox semantics survived.

**Verification:** full suite **489 tests, 489 pass, 0 fail, 0 cancelled, EXIT=0** (`.coop/logs/l3-final13.log`).
The one intermediate failure was the privilege-count gate (106 → 107, justified in place), which is the
gate working as designed rather than a regression.

**Still open in PRD-13:** the pickup OTP is minted, hashed and vaulted by `ops.visitors` and **never
handed to any rail at all** — no comms row exists — while the emitted event says "OTP sent to primary
guardian" and the audit says "OTP issued to primary guardian". Note the design trap there: returning the
code to the requesting staff member would destroy the identity check (the desk would read it to whoever
is standing there), so the honest no-carrier behaviour is to fail loudly and point at the photo-ID
fallback. Also still open from the same register row: `comms-dispatcher` resolves only the FIRST
recipient (a 300-guardian notice reaches one and still reads `sent`), and `day-worker`.

### §8 — DAT-4 + DAT-14 closed: outbox discovery is state-based (migration 076) · **2026-09-13**
One shape, two consumers, and it produced the worst kind of bug: silent, permanent data loss that every
existing signal reported as health.
- **DAT-4 — the webhook fan-out.** `scripts/webhook-worker.js` discovered work with
  `WHERE o.created_at > now() - interval '1 hour'`. A worker down for 61 minutes came back, saw nothing
  older than an hour, and never looked again, so every event in the gap was not late — it was **gone**,
  with no gap metric and no alert. The declared "at-least-once delivery spine" was at-least-once only
  while nothing went wrong for an hour.
- **DAT-14 — the workflow runner, the worse half.** The same window plus `ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 50`
  and **no cursor and no marker**, so every tick re-read the same oldest 50 rows. Past 50 matching rows
  inside the window nothing could EVER be processed: the LIMIT did not bound the work per tick, it
  bounded what could ever be done at all.
- **The fix: a per-consumer marker on the row** (`app.outbox.discovered_at`, `app.outbox.workflow_scanned_at`),
  with discovery becoming `marker IS NULL ORDER BY created_at LIMIT n` — a plain page over a partial
  index, oldest-first, that ADVANCES. A worker down for a day recovers everything when it returns; a
  worker under load can never starve a row behind the head of the queue. Markers are per CONSUMER
  because the relay, the fan-out and the runner each read the same spine independently — one shared
  "processed" flag is exactly the conflation 073 had to undo for parked rows.
- **"Considered", not "delivered"** — a deliberate semantic. A row matching no subscription is still
  marked, or an event nobody subscribes to would be rescanned every 5 seconds forever and the scan would
  stop being bounded by outstanding work. It also means a subscription created now is not handed an
  unbounded backlog of everything that ever happened, and no receiver gets a re-delivery burst because a
  stale row was re-read. Pinned by test.
- **The page is marked AFTER the work, inside the caller's transaction.** A crash mid-page rolls the
  marker back with the derived rows, so the page is re-evaluated rather than skipped. Marking first would
  have been the same silent loss in a new costume — there is a test that rolls a pass back and asserts the
  event is still UNSCANNED and fires on the next pass.
- **Three statements, not one clever CTE.** The single-statement form (a data-modifying CTE whose insert
  count is read back) reads better and is exactly the SQL that fails silently — the sibling DAT-6 work hit
  that with `FOR UPDATE`-in-a-CTE returning NULL. Each step is now obviously what it says: claim a page
  `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, schedule per matching subscription, mark the page.
- **Migration 076 backfills existing rows as already considered**, so a deploy does not hand every
  receiver the entire retained history in one burst. The guarantee it adds is about events arriving
  *after* it — which is when an outage actually happens.
- `tests/outbox-discovery.test.js` (**5/5**), written so the OLD code fails rather than so the new code
  passes: an event **three hours old** must still be scheduled (with the window restored, it fails); and
  **60** matching rows must all fire across pages — a `LIMIT 50` with no cursor cannot exceed 50 however
  many times it runs. Both gates were **verified by mutation**: restoring the window fails the first, and
  disabling the marker update fails the second with `passes were [{scanned:50,fired:50},{scanned:50,fired:0},…]`
  — the starvation signature reproduced on demand. Pre-existing `L2-4.1 · outbound webhooks` and
  `L2-5.2 · workflow step-runner` still pass unchanged (their exact counts — `enqueued === 1`,
  re-enqueue `=== 0`, `fired === 2` then `0` — are all preserved by returning the DELIVERY-row count
  rather than the rows-considered count).

### §8 — DAT-3 REFRAMED, not closed: the delivery path exists; the SIGNAL is what lies · **2026-09-13**
I set out to close DAT-3 (the relay's only subscriber is `console.log` yet the row is stamped
`processed_at`) and found the row's central claim is **false**: outbound webhook delivery is fully
implemented in `scripts/webhook-worker.js` — HMAC-signed, backoff, DLQ-as-`status='failed'`,
`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, tested by L2-4.1 — and it reads `app.outbox` **independently of
`processed_at`**, so a lost log line never lost a webhook. It now reads it with no time window at all.
Marking DAT-3 closed on the strength of my own change would have been dishonest, so it stays OPEN with a
narrower and still-real defect: the relay stamps `processed_at` for a subscriber that performs no
delivery, and the **only** queue gauge in the system (`outbox_pending`, `src/app.js`) is built on exactly
that column. The metric every operator watches therefore reports "delivered" for events that were only
written to stdout — the same success-shaped silence 073 had to undo for parked rows, in a different
column. Recorded in the register rather than silently dropped.

### §8 — DAT-6 closed (part 1): the privileged mutations that left no ledger row now do · **2026-09-13**
A security ledger is only worth what an investigator can find in it. Four high-consequence writes were
missing from it entirely, and one recorded an event while withholding the only detail that mattered.
Each fix is pinned by `tests/audit-coverage.test.js` (**7/7**), and two of its assertions are as much
about what must NOT be written as about what must.
- **`POST /me/mfa/enroll` — the one MFA transition nobody could see.** It mints a TOTP secret and
  returns it, and it wrote no row, while disabling MFA, enabling it and consuming a recovery code were
  *all* audited. So the single transition that issues a credential was the invisible one. Now recorded at
  `risk:'high'`, stating that the factor stays **INACTIVE** until a code is verified. **The secret is
  never written** — `GET /audit` is readable by principal/itadmin/finance, so a recorded secret would be
  a working credential handed to every one of them. The test asserts the returned secret *and* its
  `otpauth` URI are absent from every text column of the row.
- **The phone-OTP vault lifecycle — issue, reject, accept.** A credential was minted for an account, an
  attempt was rejected, and a session was issued on the phone factor, with nothing in the ledger, so the
  alternative sign-in factor was invisible while password logins were recorded. All three now recorded;
  the code itself never is, and only the last four digits of the phone appear (the full number is
  asserted absent — PII in an audit table readable by six roles is its own finding). The reject branch is
  bounded by design: it is unreachable once `attempts >= maxAttempts`, so a flood cannot grow the
  ledger without bound. A fourth test re-asserts the vault's **uniform failure copy** — adding logging
  must not add an oracle.
- **`GET /auth/google/callback` — silent re-pointing of how an account authenticates.** The highest
  consequence change an identity system can make, with no row at all. Now audited **on the transition
  only** (the route runs on every SSO login; logging it unconditionally would bury a genuine provider
  switch under one row per login and make `risk:'high'` meaningless).
- **`POST /plugins/:id/settings` — the de-facto credential-rotation path — recorded "settings saved"
  and named nothing.** This is where a school's `key_secret` / `webhook_secret` / SMTP password is
  replaced, and `SECRET_KEY_RE` — which exists for exactly this distinction — was never consulted here.
  The row now names the **changed keys** (never values), points at the rotation policy, and flags a
  secret-class change `risk:'high'` while an ordinary edit stays `low`. Keeping those two
  distinguishable is the entire point of recording key names.
- **A silent SQL trap found by that last test, worth knowing repo-wide.** The first version captured the
  previous settings in the same statement as a CTE referenced from `RETURNING`:
  `WITH before AS (SELECT settings … FOR UPDATE) UPDATE … RETURNING *, (SELECT settings FROM before)`.
  That returns **NULL** for the captured row — the CTE **without** `FOR UPDATE` works correctly, as does
  a plain scalar subquery in `RETURNING` (which sees the statement's pre-update snapshot), so the trap is
  specifically `FOR UPDATE` inside a CTE that the same statement's `RETURNING` reads. The failure is
  silent and one-sided: `prev = {}` would mark **every** key changed and flag **every** settings edit
  `risk:'high'` with `key_secret` named — destroying the risk column's usefulness from the opposite
  direction to the bug being fixed. Verified against PostgreSQL 18; the route now reads the old row in a
  separate `FOR UPDATE` statement, which also holds a real lock across the read-modify-write. The trap is
  documented in a comment at the call site so nobody "simplifies" it back.
- **A test that had gone vacuous, caught and repaired.** After the diff stopped returning a
  `prev_settings` field, the assertion "the response never echoes the previous settings" could no longer
  fail. It now primes a key, REMOVES it in the next save, and asserts the retired value cannot appear in
  the response — and the claim was **verified by mutation**: returning the previous row makes the test
  fail with "the previous row leaked". (My first attempt at that mutation silently edited a *different*
  route with an identical `return { plugin: r.rows[0] };` line and proved nothing — the second attempt
  asserted the mutation was in place before running.)
- **A ledger property the tests had to work around:** `created_at` is truncated to milliseconds by the
  075 writer (it must be, for a reproducible hash) and rows are tie-broken by a **random** uuid, so two
  rows written in the same millisecond have an arbitrary `id` order and "the newest row" is not. The
  test helper orders by `created_at, ctid` instead — a reliable insertion-order proxy precisely *because*
  the ledger is append-only.

### §8 — SEC-21 closed: the payment webhooks verify before they look at anything the caller controls · **2026-09-13**
- **What was wrong (two independent defects, both live).**
  1. **A pre-authentication oracle.** The Razorpay handler built its HMAC candidate list as `[env secret]` + `[the RESOLVED tenant's webhook_secret]`, and it resolved the tenant from ids **the caller supplies** (`order_log` by `razorpay_order_id`, then `app.payments` by `notes.paymentId`). In a per-tenant-secret deployment that made the pre-verification answer depend entirely on the payload: an order id resolving to a secret-bearing tenant answered **401**, while one resolving to nothing answered **403 "Payments are not set up yet"**. An unauthenticated caller could therefore enumerate which order ids exist *and* which schools have the rail configured — at two indexed owner-path queries plus a settings read per guess, with the query count as a timing signal. The 403 was reachable *because* the payload decided it.
  2. **Replay defence was opt-in by the sender.** Both rails wrote `if (ts != null)` around the freshness window, so a correctly signed body with the timestamp field simply deleted skipped the check completely — the exact replay the window exists to stop, and the gateway's own retries are indistinguishable from an attacker replaying a captured webhook.
- **The fix.** Gate 1 now derives its candidate set from the **plugin registry + env only** — never from the payload — so the pre-verification answer is payload-independent (with an env secret present the hot path touches the database **zero** times before verifying). Gate 2 makes **every** unauthenticated failure one identical response: a missing signature, a non-hex signature and a wrong one are now indistinguishable, because telling them apart is free information. Tenant resolution moved **after** verification, where a payload-dependent answer is legitimate. A missing timestamp is now a **400**, fail-closed.
- **A second vulnerability found while fixing the first, and closed with it:** nothing bound the secret that proved the event to the tenant the payload resolved to. Any party holding **one** school's webhook secret — a leaked per-school key, or a malicious tenant — could sign a body naming **another** school's order id and have the resulting fact written into that school's ledger. A per-tenant secret is now trusted only for its own tenant (a deployment-wide env secret remains trusted for any, by design), and `tests/webhook-preauth.test.js` proves both halves: the cross-tenant signature is refused **and** the correct school's signature over the same body still succeeds, so the binding cannot pass by over-blocking.
- **A cache I added and then removed, on purpose.** The first version cached the per-tenant enumeration for 5s to avoid a DB round-trip per webhook. Removed: webhook volume is a few per school per day, the enumeration is one indexed SELECT, and the staleness failure mode is a **real payment being rejected** right after an operator configures a secret — and a rotated-away secret still being *accepted* for that window. Correctness over a micro-optimisation; and because the query is payload-independent, its cost leaks nothing. (`MAX_TENANT_SECRETS = 50` with a **fail-closed** error rather than verifying against a truncated list.)
- `tests/webhook-preauth.test.js` (**8/8**) — the regression gate posts an unsigned probe naming a **real** order id and one naming a **fake** one and asserts the status **and the body are byte-identical** (on reverted code: 401 vs 403). Also pinned: no-secret-anywhere is a uniform 403 (the pre-existing fail-closed contract survives *without* becoming payload-observable), missing/non-hex/wrong signatures are indistinguishable, a correctly signed event still lands with its facts recorded (**non-vacuous**), the cross-tenant binding in both directions, no-timestamp → 400, the `body.timestamp` fallback still honoured (so the check is not simply always-fail), and a stale timestamp still → 400.
- **A test-harness trap worth knowing repo-wide:** this repo's `.env` really does define `RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, and `src/config.js` loads `.env` **at import time** — so `delete process.env.RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET` placed *before* `await import('../src/app.js')` is silently undone, the env branch wins, and every per-tenant assertion would test the wrong code path while still passing its own checks. The deletion now happens after the import and is followed by a guard assertion that fails loudly if the var is present. Recorded in `.coop/help.md`.
- **Full suite after this change: 470 tests, 469 pass, 0 fail, 1 skip, EXIT=0** (see the §8 entry below).

### §8 — SEC-20 closed: the audit ledger is now tamper-EVIDENT · **2026-09-13**
- **What was actually wrong.** `app.audit_append` validated `p_prev_hash` but stored `p_hash` **verbatim**, and `schoolos_app` holds EXECUTE on that function — so any app-role caller (any plugin, any path reachable from a request) could write a ledger row whose hash it had chosen. The row still **linked** correctly, because `prev_hash` was computed server-side, so every "is the chain intact?" check in the codebase saw nothing. Reproducing the live ledger from its own stored columns: **27 of 53 rows reproduced, 26 did not**, and for one row the stored hash matched the **migration file's** `outcome` literal rather than the `outcome` the row contained — it had been altered after insertion, and only recomputation could see it. This also **corrects a claim in this document** (§5 previously said the chain "verifies clean live" — that measured links, not hashes).
- **The fix (migration 075).** A `BEFORE INSERT` trigger (`audit_pin_hash_bi`) pins `prev_hash`, `hash`, `hash_version` and `created_at`, so the caller can no longer choose any of them. `p_hash` is now deliberately ignored and `audit.js` passes `NULL` for it. `p_prev_hash` remains only as a **conflict detector**. The xact advisory lock moved **inside** the function — it previously lived only in `src/audit.js:8`, so the 33 migration replays, `scripts/seed.js` and any future direct caller could fork the chain by reading the same `prev` concurrently. UPDATE is now refused by the **table** (`audit_ledger_immutable_bu`), not merely by the absence of a GRANT, so a future accidental grant cannot silently re-open the ledger. **DELETE is deliberately left allowed** — retention archives with `DELETE ... RETURNING` inside a CTE, so a per-row DELETE trigger would break chain-preserving archival; a deletion is instead visible to the verifier as a link break.
- **Hashing moved into the database, and the formula is versioned.** `app.audit_material()` / `app.audit_row_hash()` are the single definition, consumed by the trigger, the verifier and the tests — a second hand-written copy is how a writer and a verifier drift apart. `hash_version = 2` for rows written by the trigger; `hash_version = 1` for the pre-existing rows, which **cannot** be recomputed because the material itself changed. v1 rows are *counted, never asserted either way*: calling them verified would launder a known-bad history, calling them tampered would be a false accusation. Material uses `jsonb_build_object(...)::text` (deterministic key order, includes nulls); `json_build_object` emits spaced JSON and `jsonb_build_object` sorts keys, so a hand-written variant would never reproduce.
- **`to_char(...,'MS')` truncates microseconds**, so the trigger also does `date_trunc('milliseconds', ...)`. Without it the verifier could never reproduce the rows the trigger itself wrote — a permanent, self-inflicted false-positive generator.
- **A test found a real gap in my own fix.** The first version of the material covered the same eight fields the old JS writer used — so `reason`, `policy_ref`, `ai_involved` and `risk` could still be rewritten without disturbing the hash. The hash was over the *least interesting half of the row*: exactly the four fields an auditor reads. The material now covers every evidentiary column except the chain itself, and `ip` is included even though nothing writes it yet (0/53 rows) so that the day something does, it is covered rather than silently outside the hash. The test that caught it now pins each of the four fields individually.
- **`app.audit_verify(tenant, limit)` + `GET /audit/verify`** (owner/itadmin). The door **always returns 200** with a classified findings list — a 5xx would make "cannot verify" indistinguishable from "no problems" to any monitor that alerts only on non-2xx — and never reports a bare `ok`. It distinguishes `link_break`, `hash_mismatch` and `cold_boundary_break`, reports what it checked (`hashPinned` vs `legacyUnverifiable`), **states its own limitations** (cold chain not walked; a superuser can bypass the trigger, which is what the verifier detects *after* the fact), and **audits its own run** — a verification nobody can prove happened is not evidence. It runs on the **owner path** deliberately: `app.audit_log_cold` is `REVOKE ALL` from the app role, so a `withUser` implementation would read nothing there and still report a clean bill of health.
- `tests/audit-integrity.test.js` (**14/14**) — the regression gate is the first test: it calls `audit_append` as the app role with `p_hash='deadbeef'` and asserts the stored hash is **not** that. On reverted code it stores `deadbeef`. Also pinned: the link is computed rather than accepted (and a genuinely stale `prev_hash` still raises), millisecond truncation, DB-side UPDATE refusal on the owner path *and* the app path, detection of a forged after-the-fact rewrite as exactly one `hash_mismatch` with no spurious `link_break`, detection of a deleted row as a link break, v1 rows counted but not asserted, the tenant pin on `audit_verify` (a cross-tenant call is **refused**, not merely empty), per-tenant isolation with a second tenant, and the door's refusal of teacher/parent plus `?limit=abc|-1|0|1e9` never 500ing.
- **Two existing tests encoded the superseded hashing and were moved with the fix, not around it:** `tests/security.test.js` now asks the **database** for the formula and additionally asserts the old JS material does *not* reproduce a pinned row; `tests/l2-retention-dayworker.test.js` now builds genuinely chained rows through `audit_append` instead of raw INSERTs with literal `'prev-old'`/`'hash-old'` (which the trigger would silently rewrite, leaving the test asserting the *absence* of its own fixtures) and asserts both `prev_hash` **and** `hash` are byte-identical across the hot→cold move, plus that the move does not restamp `hash_version`.
- **Migration bookkeeping, stated plainly:** 075 was applied, then **finalized and re-applied** within the same session after the material-coverage gap was found. This is normally forbidden (the checksum drift guard exists for good reason), and it was safe only because the ledger held **zero v2 rows** at that moment — verified by query before editing, so no row was written under a superseded formula and no data repair was needed. Recorded in `.coop/help.md`. Anything older would have required a follow-up migration instead.

### §8 — SEC-2b closed in full, SEC-4 solved differently than the register proposed, SEC-5 gated · **2026-09-13**
- **SEC-2b (UPDATE/SELECT half) CLOSED** — `migrations/077_acl_column_scope.sql` + `tests/acl-column-scope.test.js` (8/8).
